information brought to my mind some local gossip I had heard two years previously when staying at the house of Colonel Hill, near King's Lynn. In one way er another I heard of the active interest you have taken in Peacock Farm for years past, of your advice as regards lavish expenditure for so-called improvements and expensive agricultural speculations in the time of the late Mr. Gresham, and of how you encouraged him to raise on the place loans which he could never repay. And I learnt that, after the death of Mr. Gresham, when you became manager of the property, you continued the same course of action. Mr. Gresham had been too trustful, and these ladies could not be expected to undoistand our plotting.", "Sir, do you mean to say--" "11.n= tna to proceed uninterrunfitd. if you please,— slialunieign, calmly, tnougn in a tone that compelled compliance. 4A week ago I was the spectator of a dramatic incident which at once roused me to action. In consequence, lon Monday last travelled to London, and when there had a long interview with Messrs. Wilton and Esker, solicitors, who held the mortgages put on this place by you. I had sorrowful reason to know that firm—of whose methods I need not express an opinion—for nearly twenty years ago, when I was a lad of seventeen, my poor father's little estate near Drayton, in this county, was sold up by them, wherethey mercilessly foreclosed the mortgages they had encouraged him to put on his property. It may not interest you to learn that both my father and mother died shortly afterwards of grief; but it may interest you to be told that I have discovered that the very ener- getic member of that firm who did their dirty work on the occasion I have mentioned was then named Ward, although this name has since grown into Cooper-Ward." That individual now stared at Ashleigh with blank, stupid astonishment. "And now, Mr. Cooper-Ward, I have just one final piece of information for you before you go—which will be at once, as your trap awaits you at the door—and that is that the necessary price which you persuaded these ladies could never be obtained for Peacock Farm has been paid—paid, sir—and the mort- gages bought up. Having informed you on this important point, Mr. Cooper-Ward, I shall now take the liberty of telling you that you are a , scoundrel, unfit for the company of these ladies •or even of me, who have met many scoundrels; and that if you don't depart at once, never again to shew your face here, I shall thrash zou out of the house."

Mr. Cooper-Ward's puffy face went grey under its usually indelible purple, and he glared fiercely at Ashleish.

'-uht Out—immediately!" said the latze— sternly, and he stepped forward and raised the riding crop suggestively as he spoke. And then the bully, without a word, slunk out of the room. There were some minutes of happy silence after that, Mrs. Gresham weeping for sheer joy, and her daughter gazing with glowing eyes at Ashleigh. Then he was the first to speak. "My change of name may hive puzzled you," he said, "but that occurred through the accident of my giving only half my name when I was interrupted. Afterwards when I heard myself called Mr. Herbert,' ?accepted the change, as I desired quietness, and I might have been the cause of inflicting visitors on you if certain people in this district had known I was here." "Ah! now I remember hearing of Colonel Hill's guest, Mr. Ashleicrh. the great ship- owner," said Mrs.. Gresham. "And you actually paid us a visit? How did I not remember you!' wore a beard then, owing to a temporary delicacy of the throat." There was again a pause in the conversation, everyone at the moment feeling too deeply moved for words. Then, in answer to some half-query from Mrs. Gresham, Ashleigh said: "My wealth I attained to only after many long years of uphill work, the story of which I must tell you some day. But now the strain is over and I may take my ease in the country, if I prove acceptable, and indulge to the full my hobby of landseapelmintini." "You have bought Beacock Farm! faltered Miss Gresham.

THE CULT OF SUICIDE. Encouraged by the Breakneck Speed

of Life.

Suicide has become something of the nature of a cult. Its popularity (writes the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, vicar of i Burtonwood) s great, and homage is paid to it as to a deity. Soon we shall hear of it erecting its altars, consecrating its priests and developing a system of religious observances. The most painful features in connection with it are appnrent infatuation it possesses for the young, the careful premeditation, and the almost religious deter- mination with which self-murder is committed. Young men hurl themselves befoles,fast-flying trains, and young women fling themselves into the canals with all the sang /raid of accomplished actors. It must needs be that brains will crack and minds will swerve living as we do at motor speed. Twentieth century speed is doing no end of injury to the youth of the country ; it is beginning to tell seriously on the nervous system of our young manhood and womanhood. We are becoming terribly neurotic. Life is not worth living now unless the speed is high. Two things ever necessary to life, to health and longevity, are absent from us to-day—quiet and commonsense.

PROSPERITY IN THE CYCLE TRADE.

The reports recently issued to shareholders in Cycle Companies have in nearly every instance shown a most satisfactory state of prosperity largely due to the increased use of the low priced bicycle by the great massrs of the population. The latest report of this kind that comes to hand is from the New Hudson Cycle Company Limited, %%filch shows a net profit for the year of £20.470 105..9d. on a paid up capital of only .£58,672. The directors pay a dividend of 10 per cent. end a bonus of 5 per cent. and these payments are on the original capital as subscribed by the shareholders not as in many cases upon a capital greatly reduced by losses. FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. Rev. John Yonge's Thirty Years'

Ministry.

THE ROMAN CATHOLIC CHAPLAIN AT

THE WORKHOUSE.

A meeting of the Warrington and District Free Church Council, at which several matters of public interest were under consideration, was held in the vestry of 80ld-street Wesleyan Church on Monday evening. Mr. Charles E. Parker presided over a large and representative attendance, including the lteir..l. P. Matthews (one of the secretaries).

eduoation controversy the present was hardly a fitting: juncture to make a protest which might create some feeling. The meeting shared this view and no definite action was agreed upon for the present.

WEEK OF UNIVERSAL PRAYER. It was decided to observe the week of universal prayer from January 6th to January 13th. The annuli interchange of pulpits was also arranged for the same month.

A MILD AND GENIAL MEDICINE.

A Scottish Merchant Praises .the Natural Composition of Bile Beans,

THE EDUCATION BILL. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS said an urgent whip had been sent out by the National Council to all the secretaries asking them to bring before their respective Councils the fact that a meeting was likely to be con- vened for November sth of all the delegates who were present at the Birmiugham.Conference to decide what action the Free Church Council should take in view of the prospective action of the House of Lords in con- nection with the Education Bill. It was requested that the Council should select delegates. Mr. Parker, and he (Mr. Matthews) were the delegates to the Birmingham meeting but as he (Mr. Matthews) was not in sympathy with the attitude of the Free Church Council in regard to simple Bible teaching, it would, be better if the meeting elected a delegate who could honestly vote for the proposals of the National Council. After discussion it was decided that the President should represent the Council at the meeting. PROPOSED FREE CHURCH PARISHES. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS said that a little while ago, as the result of the open-air campaign, a meeting of the open air workers and members of the committee was summoned to consider what issues they might place before the Free Church Council as the outcome of their united work. It was decided to urge upon the Free Church Council the desirability of concentrating their efforts as much as possible during the coming winter upon their own churches,and to that end it was very desir- able that there should be Free Church parishes formed in Warrington, certain districts assigned to each Church, and systematic visitation undertaken. As far as the ministers were concerned, he believed their ptactically unanimous consent would be forthcoming. The Rev. JOHN Yoatoal said the chief aim was to bring the churches into contact with people sche did not attend any place of worship. The arrangement would, he thought, stimulate work and concentrate their efforts. He moved : "That the Warrington Free Church Council cordially approves Of the scheme of the National Council for the formation of Free Church parishes for the purposes of domiciliary visitation, the object being that of bringing the Gospelinto touch with such families as are not at present in connection with any particular church. That for the carrying into effect of this resolution a committee be appointed to prepare a scheme for the delimitation of a parish to each of the Free Churches." , The Rev. SIDNEY PITT seconded and the proposal was unanimously agreed to. It was decided that the committee should consist of all the ministers together with representatives of each church which had not a minister and that a map defining the parishes should be prepared. It was also agreed to direct the special attention of the churches to lodging-house services and visitation, there being a general opinion that particular efforts should be made in that direction. THE REV. JOHN YONGE'S COMPLETION OF THIRTY YEARS' MINISTRY. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS said the Rev. John Yonge completed thirty years' ministry at Wycliffe Church on Sunday next. Such a long period of service in a town like Warrington spoke well for the man and equally well for the charge over which God had set him and be (Mr. Matthews) did not think the haembers of that Council would be doing themselves justice ' if they did not take notice in a formal way of a ministry so long, so valuable, so distinctly brotherly, healthy and helpful as Mr. Yonge's had been. He moved, with the warmest and deepest feelings possible, the following resolu- tion:—" That this Warrington and District Free Church Council congratulates the Rev. John Yonge on his long and conspicuously able and useful ministry in Warrington. His share in public life has been large, and his influence for good very great. This meeting heartily thanks God for him and his work, and expresses the ho_pe that it will be continued for a\ long time to come. It begs to assure Wycliffe Church of its warm affection for Mr. Yonge, and joins its payers with those of his people for the continuance of hi 4 happy and efficient ministry. It would join in all this Mrs. Yonge's name with that of her husband." The Rev. SIDNEY PITT said it was a matter of great gratification to him to second the resolution. He was thankful that the watchfulness of their Secretary had given them the opportunity of expressing the feeling which was in all their minds. As a near neighbour of Mr. Yonge's ecclesiastically, he had learned to esteem him and his work very highly, and to recognise in him a very wise and capable leader of the Nonconformist forces in this town. He wished Mr. and Mrs. Yonge many years to be spent among their own people, and that when the evening did come round " at eventide it might be light." The Rev. F. Viuostaasin supporting, said that when he came to Warrington as a total stranger the second ministerial hand that gripped his was that of Mr. Yonge. He had not been long in the town before he found that Mr. Yonge occupied a very conspicuous position in the life of the locality and that he controlled in a very important sense much of the religious thought of very many of God's people. Mr. Yonge's personality and work commanded his (Mr. Vaughan's) respect and devotion. Mr. PARKES supported on behalf of the Primitive Methodist Churches. . The. CHAIRMAN said that for twenty five years Mr. Yonge and he had been more or less associated. He had known Mr. Yonge to be a strong Congregationalist but his Congregationalism had not been in any way of a narrow type, for he had given of his strength and services most lavishly to all the other Nonconformist Churches. (Hear, hear.) He had been a strong Non- conformist; he had been their leader; and they had always felt that their interests were perfectly safe in his hands and that while he would ever stand loyal to principle he waS ready as occasioned offered to unite in any Christian work with the Established Church. The resolution was heartily passed. The Rev. JOHN YONGE, in respondinr, said that while deeply sensible of the exceeding kindness which had prompted that expression, it was very difficult for him to respond in anything like adequate or appropriate terms. The fact of one's having been minister of a Church for thirty years was a somewhat exceptional circumstance and he must confess that if he had any pride at all, he had pride in that circumstance. If Mr. Pitt would not mind his saying so, he did not envy a Wesleyan Minister one bit. (Laughter.) There Was a great charm about a continued residence in a definite flock for a anusiderable period, and when the time came for him to hand over the ' reins of Wycliffe Church to a younger man, it would be to both Mrs. Yonge and himself a greater wrench than he could describe. He was very grateful that what he had Reports from reliable sources show that recent, been enabled to do had won for him the esteem end i weather vagaries have produced quite an exc ptionat respect of the Free Churches of the town. That the Bile Bean way of ending disorders of stomach, liver, and bowels is far superior to that ofi, other medicines has received ample illustration recently..i Mr. William McWhiner, a retired merchant living at " ) 23, Wallace-street, Ayr, Scotland, says :—" I can, strongly recommend Bile Beans, for I have never had - any medicine that acted so mildly and yet so effica- ciously. My complaint was constipation, and the many medicines I tried on former occasions entirely failed to bring relief in the same mild manner as Bile ireins.

"I have strong. faith in purely herbal or vegetable medicines, and it is a great satisfacteon to me to know that Bile Beans do not contain chemicals or drugs of any kind. For mineral drugs hive so often not only disappointed but have wrought harm and havoc rather than curd.

"I told my wife that medicines of herbal or vege- table composition were undoubtedly the best, and though she is not easily persuaded to change her medi- cine, I prevailed upon her to take Bile Beans, which she did, with success. I shall recommend Bile Beans when- ever I can." ' Mr. McWhiner is quite right in his statement that mineral drugs, as contained in many of the medicines sold to-day,do more harm than good. They may act as a stimulant and force activity in the sluggish organs, but this activity is unnatural, and when the effects have passed away a worse state of weakness prevails than befOre.

The Bile Bean method is quite different. Bile Beans are composed of the purest and most potent of Nature's herbs, roots, and barks. The natural properties of Bile Beans set right the processes of digestion and nutrition in the disordered organs, so gently and genially that their action is scarcely perceptible, and with none of the drastic after-effects that follow the taking of drugs and mineral Medicines. All chemists retail Bile Beaus in sealed boxes, at Is. id. and 2s. 9d. (2s. 9d. size contains 3 times quantity of smaller box).

ST. PETER'S CHURCH. Annual Sale of Work.

Success attended the annual sale of work in connec- tion with St. Peter's Church, which was held in the schoolroom, John-street on Monday and Tuesday. In this large and poor parish, outside help is necesFary for the maintenance of the many useful organizations, and each year great interest is taken in the sale which Is always well supported by the parishioners themselves and friends from other parts of town. Thanks to the efforts of a large and willing band of workers, the stalls were laden with an abundance of wares and the room was very attractively arranged. The following acted as stallholders :—Vicarage stall, Mesdames Percival, John Taylor, Joseph Taylor, Heacock, Greaves East, and Davison ; congregational stall, Mesdames Edgar, and Cowdell, Misses Webster, and Annie Edgar ; flower stall, Misses Wilson, Eva raylor, and Alice Taylor ; refreshment stall, Mrs. Bamforth. Mrs. Lingley, Mrs. Webster,Mrs.Skelland, Miss Barlow and Miss Stanley; toy stall, Misses Lily Percival, Mabel Horton, A. Kenyon and Gertrude Wilson; sweet stall, Miss Nellie Hughes and Miss Percival ; bran tub, Mies Nellie Percival.

The sale was opened by Mrs. Coxon, of Lymm, wt, performed that pleasing duty in the absence of 3111 Charlton Parr.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The bazaar was re-opened on Tuesday by Mrs. Peter Rylands, who was introdaced by the Vicar as a ladY " who took delight in doihg good." (Applause). Mrs. RYLANDS said she was sure thay were all present that day to make the sale a great success. It must bq a difficult matter in a parish like that, with so maul poor people in it, to find suiliciedt money necessary to work it. Therefore it was obvious that it was necessary to go outside for help, and compel themto render assist- ance. She was glad to hear that they did fairly well on the previous day, and she hoped they would do well that day also, and that the sum required would be raised. She had much pleasure in declaring the sale open (Applause)., Mrs. Rylands wee warmly thanked for her services, on the motioa of Mr. BAMFORTH, seconded by the Rev. H. RUSSELL.

The tots.' proceeds of the sale amounted to £122.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King's Birthday Party.—The house party at andringham for the Kings birthday festivities is 'always a small one, and beyond the more immediate members of his Majesty's own family circle, usually 'consists of a few of their Majesties.' most intimate friends. Lady de Grey, who like the Marquis de Soveral, stands in high favour at Court, is invariably included in these gatherings. She was formerly the Countess of Lousdale, and although now grey-haired,

she is still one of the most beautiful women in -Society: She was left a widow very early in life, and married in 1885, as her second husband, Earl de Grey, who is the only sou of the Marquis of Ripon. The Royal house party also includes Lord and Lady Farquhar, the Duke and Duchess of Portland, Lord and Lady Colebrooke, and a few others devoted to sport, for whom the shoot- inng at Sandringham unvariably provides some splendid

- . . bags. Horsewomen arkl Riding Habits.—The Duchess of Westminster and Lady Castlereagh. are both great horsewomen, and are quoted as being in favour of women riding astride in the hunting field. There could be no better authorities than these two ladies on the relative advantages of the cross and side saddle, but although there may be something to be said for the greater safety of the cross saddle, very few among well-known horsewomen have ventured to try it. Certainly there is nothing to be said for it on the score of appearance, and, as regards safety, opinions are about equally divided. Many hold that a good horsewoman IS more secure on a properly equipped side saddle, than a man is on his. It is said that accidents in the hunting field are more frequent among women than men, but this also is questioned, and some declare that it is the Other way about, because a mishap to a man often passes unnoticed, whereas more is made of it if it is a woman Who comes to grief. In any case the matter of appear- ance is quite enough to decide the question as it did in deciding whether women should adopt the rational costume for cycling. There can be no two opinions that this was a more convenient style of dress for cycling, but it did not look well, neither does a woman on a cross saddle, and that is an all sufficient reason why riding habits will not be dispensed with. A Leading Victorian Hostess.—Lady Dorothy Nevill, who has just given us an interesting book of memoirs "Trifling Reminiscences" (Arnold) was one of the great Society leader* of the Victorian era. The daughter of the Earl of Oxford. she was born in 1826, and with perhaps the exception of Lady Burdett-Cgutts, there is no one living, who had a more intimate acquaintance with all the great celebrities of Queen Victoria's reign. The Primrose League was fonnded at her luncheon table, but Lady Dorothy has been always broad-minded, in her political views, and the leading men of all parties were among her intimate friends. ._She knew well the Groat Duke of Wellington, Lord Deaconsfleld, Mr. Gladstone, Lord Rindolnh Churchill, Mr. Cobden, Lord Salisbury, and among the living, Mr. Chamberlain, Mr. i;alfour, and in fact every one of note in the politieal arena. But all people of light and leading, whether in politics, art, law, literature, or the stage met under her roof, and among her especial friends were Lord Tennyson and Sir Henry Irving. Lady Dorothy herself possesses real literary talent and her book of memoits is full of delightful anecdotes. She is still quite active interesting herself in philan- thropic work, and going out a great deal, although she says there is a vast difference, between the Society of to-day, and as she knew it in earlier life. About Furs.—The woman who sallies forth in search of furs needs to exercise her judgment to a considerable degree, especially if the sum at her command is com- paratively small. When faked furs became fashionable, it was not difficult to foretell a time when a greet deal of rubbish would find its way into the market : that time has certainly arrived, and we need to be careful. To the majority of women sable, chinchilla, ermine, marten and fox, are quite unattainable luxuries, and thr ir choice therefore lies between imitations of these leading furs, " faked specimens," or the inexpensive real furs. Unquestionably the latter selection is the best from every point of view. Take for example bear. A neat flat stole with real tail ends, and a muff to . .

match, are possessions which will wear splendidly, and of which a woman need never feel ashamed. It is true that bear is not considered a fashionable fur, but after lan it is only the women whose dress allowance runs int three figures, who need worry about this particular , decree. The same rule should hold good in purchasing fur, as when buying lace, namely, that one yard of the best of its kind, is worth a furlong of a cheap imitation. A really good piece of lace, or fur, is a delight to its possessor all her life. The "gad() " Mn ff.—The home worker who perchance has a fur stole, and no muff to match, or perhaps a muff that has seen its best days, should experience little difficulty in bringing her winter costumes up-to-date provided she has had the forethought to provide herself with a yard or two of material for emergencies. First of all, the shape of the muff—which should be of a large flat variety—must be cut in stiff tailor's canvas, then covered inside and out with thick layers of Wadding.—Next the exact thane (outside) should be taken in thin lining, and upon this the material which snatches the costume, should be artistically draped, and frills of the same sewn near the edges. Then strips of the fur either new, to match the boa, or taken from the discarded muff, should be laid next the frills, and towards the centre, and the whole" creation " slipped over the foundation, the edges turned inside, and fixed. Last of all, a muff lining—wide and inexpensive selections are to be found in all the large shops—should be tacked in, care being taken that the lining repeats the relieving colour in the bat and costume. Even though the strips of fur have to be bought, such a muff as the one des- • cribed costs little besides the trouble for which the worker will find herself repaid. " Made" muffs are having a great vogue, even among those women who are the happy possessors of priceless furs. Brocade Fashionable.—Brocade:is particularly modish and the most exquisite patterns, carried out upon shot , grounds in silvery colourings, are available. The Princess of Wales has always had a great affection for brocade and the return to favour of this lovely fabric, is in great measure duo to Her Royal Highness's prefer- ence for it. Needless to say the Princess only chooses brocade of home manufacture, and design,and we should see to it that we follow her example.

- - - Dearer Soap.—Dear soap did not trouble out ancestors very much, because like the ancient Britons, People did without it. hi the time of the Charles's

''`

I soap making was a monopoly and the State exa otul a Iheavy toll upon it. It did upon most things in those days although Charles IL used to say that he eaved the country a good deal of money by the large sums he won from his friends at cards. Taxes on soap, how- ever, were continued down to the year 1 853, which appears to have been about the time when people began to wash themselves. Since then, soap has become more indispensable, a fact to which the din raised over the Soap Trust testifies, and the sympathy of the public will be entirely on the side of those who hope it will be defeated in the endeavour to reduce weights and raise prices. It may not make much difference to each individual household,although the laundries ara already talking of raising their prices, but cheap soap is one of the blessings of modern times,and it does not appear that the great soap making firms were doing so badly that they must needs seek to exploit the pubiic to add to their already enormous profits.

LADY DOROTHY NEVILL.

Lady Dorothy Nevill may be described as one of the institutions of London, and a popular one at that. A daughter of the third Earl of Orford. Lady Dorothy as girl, wife, and widow travelled much, learnt to know men and cities, and made the acquaintance of many celebrities. She de- scribes her recreations as "horticulture, botany, and the fine arts," but in point of fact her favourite recreation is entertaining. Her house in Charles-street, Berkeley-square, is one of the best-liked in London, and her, Sunday luncheons are brilliantly successful. Smart, clever, and lovely women, painters, preachers, and politi- cians, actors, judges, and journalists, soldiers, sailors—all people of light and leading meet under her hospitable root London has beep de- scribed as one long, whispering gallery to Lady Dorothy Nevill. What • she does not know is not worth knowing; but such is her discretion, says the Reader, that she never tells a tale to the wrong person She ' is perhaps the greatest woman wit of her time, and everywhere from Sandringham downwards, she is a welcome guest. Her quaint mob cap and rows of amber beads are a familiar eight in the smartest draw- ing rooms of London.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
PRETTY DRESSING- JACKET.

Pictured in our illustration is a charming and cosy little dressing-jacket, suitable for • wear either as a matinee, a bed-jacket in case of ill- ness, or as a best dressing-jacket. The material of which it is fashioned is Ivory-white cashmere, a fabric which washes and wears beautifully. The dainty little garment is quite loose, and rather full, the fulness being taken into three deep tucks on either shoulder, each tuck being firmly machined or feather-stitched down to hold it in position. Round the neck and down the fronts runs a band of embroidery, worked in

white or coloured silks, according to the taste of the prospective owner, and edged with lines of thick outline stitch, worked in heavy silk. Tiqe sleeves, of the modified cape shape, terminate just above the wrist, and are edged with bands of similar embroidery

Dr.Willianis' Pink Pills.

1 Mr. John Thompson is the energetic and popular secretary in Liverpool of an important trade union, and when he consented to state his personal experience for publication his kindly motive was explained by the following remarks : "As I go to work each morning I pass dozens of fellow- workers who plainly are ill. Anyone could tell that they are in the grip of disease, and I would be glad to do them the good turn that a friend once did me. When I had looked on myself as a' goner ' beyond human aid a friend advised me to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and they saved my life ; so I advise every man who earns his daily bread to keep these pills handy. Mr. Thompson, who is a skilled joiner, living at 49, Corney-street, Smithdown-road, Livergool, continued : "My illness began with a depressed feeling; a kind of washed-out-ever-tired sensation hung over me. I felt lifeless and drowsy; yet sleep, when I went to bed, never came to me. This was followed by severe chest pains, and at last a terribly hard cough that would attack me suddenly if I walked at a quick pate or went upstairs. This cough racked me to pieces, and made my chest terribly raw. When the winter mornings came and I went into the cold air neighbours around would hoar me cough- ing as though my lungs were in shreds. I felt myself that I could not last. No one held out hope and doctors, although they did their best, seemed unable to give rue just the medicine my illness required. I was sinking fast. I tried to keep up my strength, but I had a most wretched feeling in my stomach, and for at least two hours after every meld I felt that food would be rejected. So I began to loathe food, and my appetite dwindled away. I became a stooping, weak-backed, pale, consumptive-looking creature of whom everybody remarked 'Heis in a deline.' My sleep was broken by frightful dreams, and many hours I lay awake bathed in weakening perspirations. For days together I could not move owing to nervous headaches. My cough had now become so bad that every fit prostrated me. Nervous, easily excited and flurried, I had to remain away from work. I looked haggard, while my flesh bad fallen away greatly. " A fellow-workman, who had great faith in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People earnestly begged Ime to try them. Presentation to Mr. T. P. Cargill.

Mr. T. P. Cargill, engineer for Messrs. N. Greening and Sons, Limited, who is leaving Warrington to take up important duties in Scotland was last week the recipient of a handsome token of esteem from his fellow employees. Mr. Webster, in making the pre- sentation, said they were sorry Mr. Cargill was leaving them, as they knew he was a man who would be very difficult to replace. He had great pleasure in pre- senting a secretaire on behalf of all present, and he took that opportunity to wish him every success, God's blessing, and good health go with him and his family.. (Applause.) Cheers for Mr. and Mrs. CargiM were given, followed by the singing of "He's a jolly good fellow 4" After the ceremony the officials adjourned m the Winmarleigh Cafe for dinner, Mr. Webster presiding. An excellent repast was provided and the usual toasts were sub.nitted.

St. Elphin's 0, Latchford Crescent 3. Castner Reserve 3, Warrington•St. Paul's 0. Albions Reserve 3, Penketh 3. -- SANKEY Sr. MARY'S V. OLD BOTELRRIANS.

This match was played at Sankey on Saturday last and resulted in an easy victory for the visitors by S goals to 2. The game had been in progress but a few moments when the visitors scored. This caused the home team to rally, and amid great excitement and the cheers of their own supporters, they equalised shortly afterwards. The game subsequently went all in favour of the Old Botelerians who out-played their opponents in every department of the game and won as stated. The goals for the visitors were scored by Lowe (3), Nield (3), Percival (1) and one by a member of the Sankey team. Haigh scored both goals for Sankey. The victory of the Botelerians was due to the existence of a perfect understanding between each individual member of the team while the Sankey players had apparently not had sufficient practice together.

THE marriage service, it

THE DILEMMA has been observed, begins •OF THE PEERS. with "dearly beloved " and

ends with "amazernent."We are reminded of the witticism by the present con- duct of the House of Lords. An ancient body,the oldest of our Parliaments, began its manipulation of the Education Bill in a spirit of gay confidence, from which, finding that it had to deal with matters of business among other things, it has plunged into a state of amazed confusion. The country, which is to be taught a lesson, for the reason that it has sent a tremendous Liberal majority to Parliament, is to bo taught, first of all, that the House of Lords is much more intimately acquainted with what it wants than is the House of Commons. It is to be taught in the second place, that his Majesty's Ministers have not been able to make their Bill what they intended it to be, and that the Lords aro not less the inter- preters of the mind of the Government than of the will of the people. This is all very delighaul, or at least it would be delightful if we could treat it as the pretty make-believe of children. Certainly, it is a play carried out in that spirit, and with the same careless feeling of irresponsibility. Confronted with unfamiliar things, the Peers get into a most astonishing tangle about their own amendments, and have to adjourn at an earlier hour when they contemplated a late one, because they cannot understand what they are doing or what they are trying to do. One tangle has followed upon another. Their lordships, believing vastly in themselves all the while, have plunged from bewilderment to bewilderment ; they don't seem in the;least aware of what they have done. There will be no more amazed persons in the world than themselves when they find they have destroyed the Edumaion Bill. They have no such desperate intention; they want the Bill very badly, if they can only get it with their own trimmings, and they want it—at least, the Bishops want it— whether with trimmings or without. To destroy the Bill would be to forego many substantial advantages with regard to voluntary schools. The I[Bishops are eager to have these schools finally taken over by the public authorities. This fact comes out almost plaintively now and then. But they imagine that they can obtain what they want in this respect, and a great deal more besides. Also they imagine that the settlement of the education question depends on their will, and that the House of Commons can do only what they per- mit it to do. A more exaggerated spectacle was never presented to an amused world. It is the fashion, for the time being, to speak of the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY as an extra- ordinarily astute person. For our own part we ' cannot perceive that Dr. DAVIDSON is entitled to even this dubious sort of praise. He is,we admit, expending a great deal of ingenuity in attempts to thwart the purposes of the House of Commons, but as it is ingenuity that must be ultimately thrown away, it does not appear to be worthy of special admiration. Besides, it is an ingenuity that is all the time defeating its own ends. The Government is nut under the necessity of accept- ing any of the Lords' amendments for the sake of saving its Bill, because it can attain its end even if it permits the Bill to sink into the limbo of abortive schemes, which is the last thing that the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY and his friends desire.

There are other alternatives. One of them is pointed out by a shrewd correspondent of the " Westminster Gazette." The proper course for the House of Commons to adopt when the Lords have ruined tho Education Bill, he says, is to pass a resolution declaring that no school shall be recog- nised as a public elementary school if any portion of the school hours on any day is set apart for purposes of denominational education. This resolution, followed up by a reduction of the education vote,so as to deprive all denominational schools of the Government grant, is entirely within the rights of the House of Commons. And so it is—a fact of which the House of Lords, with its bench of Bishops, has evidently failed to take note.

It is not yet possible to say A FORECAST or what lines the Government TEMPERANCE Licensing Bill will take,but LEGISLATION. • SOMii deductions may be drawn from a group of significant circumstances. The two sections of the Temperance party have still their different points of view. The United Kingdom Alliance is less heroic than the Temperance Legislation League. Sir T. P. Whittaker' s optimism went nearly to poetry when the latter organisation opened its campaign the other night. A "wide local option," a restrinion of the stimulus to private enterprise by reducing the profits, and free clubs through the land conducted upon a temper- ance basis—there is the Utopian aspect of a pro- gramme outlined by a progressive reformer. But Sir Thomas favours "disinterested management." The United Kingdom Alliance does not. Indeed, it is said to deprecate the introduction of this principle into the bill. But there are points on which they agree, and three of them, it is safe to conclude, will be set in the forefront of the forthcoming measure. These are the time limit, the restoration and enlargement of the powers of local licensing magistrates and the control of clubs. On other items there is substantial agreement; but opinions differ as to means. The League would leave them to local option; the Alliance apparently prefers direct

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

legislation. These reforms are shorter hours, closing on Sundays and all election days, and the abclition of barmaids. There is plenty of variety here, but one may discern, at any rate, amid so many demands, the emergence of a policy. Much more will doubtless be heard of it during the coming winter. The Temperance Legislation League :is promoting meetings in all the large towns in the next month or two, and the first word of advocates in all cases will probably be a moving appeal for unity.

The Town Council has pro- THE CARNEGIE visionally accepted Mr. DONATION. Carnegie's offer of £3,500 for a branch library in War- rington. There was a good deal of trouble caused by the word "provisionally," some members who ought to have known better quite failing to grasp the import and significance of the word. They seemed to think that accepting the offer " provisiOnally was equivalent to accepting it altogether and saying" thank you." As a matter of fact the Council is still at liberty to decline the offer if it thinks fit. Mr. Carnegie's munificence was by no means referred to in glowing terms. Thirty-five hundred pounds may represent a con- siderable sum but it is not much for branch libraries in a town like Warrington. Alder- man Tionion expressed the opihion that the committee should ask for more, and we believe they have done so as deli- cately as possibly. They are fully aware, however, that it is not wise to look a gift horse too closely in the mouth. Mr. Carnegie has given Blackpool—a much smaller town than War- rington—no less than £15,000, and Warrington has therefore a right to complain. It ought to be made clear that unless the millionaire sends enough of his cash to make a decent job of the branch library scheme we will have none of it. A proper pride in these matters goes a long way. Being not too proud to beg, let Warrington show sufficient pride to beg for enough!

Perhaps the most surprising THE ELECTION feature of Thursday's BALANCE SHEET. election results in War-

rington is that all the retir- ing councillors who went to the poll were, with one exception, re-elected. St. John's Ward was the only one which abandoned its sitting member Mr. John Maginn, who three years ago had the enormous majority of 641 and is now defeated by 5:22. Mr. Maginn may well reflect on the fickleness of the voter, and we believe he will be widely sympathised with in his misfortune. The Council has lost an energetic, fearless, and useful member, and we : hope it will not be long before Mr. Magibn finds his way back to the representative chamber. Naturally, with so few changes, the balance of power between the different parties in the Council is only very slightly disturbed. The Conserva- tives have gained one seat—that of St. John's— the Liberals have lost the same together with con- siderable prestige elsewhere, the Labour party has had to give up Bewsey, and the Non-Traders have rather more than held their own. This is roughly the position of affairs as the result of the seven contests on Thursday, and anything less revolutionary could hardly have been forecasted. There are points of political interest and municipal moment which, howevßr, cannot be overlooked though they are not indicated by the bare results.

Owing to the defeat of THE NON-TRADERS, Mr. _Dodds in Latchford

AND LATCHFORD. Ward, many people have hastily assumed that the Non-Traders hava received their death-blow. Such is, we are glad to say, far from being the case. As a direct result of the elections the position of the Non-Traders in the Town Council is stronger than aver it was. All but two of the nine wards have returned a representative pledged to oppose any self-seeking in the Council Chamber—a wonderful testimony to the good work which the Non-Traders have done and are still doing. The chief excep- tion is in Latchford itself,where Councillor Wilson will still, possibly,continue to administer with one hand, and pocket the ratepayers' monies by virtue of his services as mayor's auditor, with the other. His position is, in our opinion, totally indefensible and we regret that his suppoeters, as well as himself,have been so blind and oblivious to the real issue. It wont.' be idle to deny that all supporters of the purity movement are deeply disappointed at Mr. Dodds' failure to secure election on this occasion. He is a roan whose services in the Town Council would have been extremely valuable and one would have liked to see the triumph once again of principles over party and personalities. Mr. Dodds was not fighting for the sake of securing office, but in defence of the gnat priaciple that no man shall make profit out of his service to the ratepayers. Fortunately there is no danger of the position taken up by himself and his supporters being weakened by the fact that the electors of Latchford have proved false to the trust reposed in them. All through this particular contest the question at stake was obscured by a tangle of calumnies and misrepresentations which have obviously been gulped down holes boles by the unthinking voters. We are confident, howevec, that right will prevail, and Mr. Dodds deserves the thanks of the people of Warrington for having under such difficult circumstances upheld the banner of public probity and moral rectitude high I above the mi7e of man's meanness and the murk of misunderstanding.

. The fight in Latchford—as MALICR AND on a previous occasion—has MEN, revealed some of the depths to which men will sink in order to gratify a personal and petty spite. There are concrete evidences to base such an assertion upon and we do not intend to mince matters in dealing with them, On the whole Mr. Dodds' principal opponents, the Conservatives of Latchford, fought fairly, above board, and for party politics before anything else. So long as politics are inseparable from municipal contests we shall always respect an honest party fighter. What we abhor is the deliberate sacrifice of all principle by men who, iu the past, h we had some reputation fer honour among their fellows. Such beings there are in Latchford, and, grevious to confess, they are called Liberals.. They helped to swell the majority of Mr. Wilson, a pronounced Tory, but not because Mr. Dodds was anything but a true Liberal so far as imperial politics are concerned. No, there was an undercurrent of deadly hate begotten by an evil conscience, which influenced the action of a contemptible few. All of them cannot be identified here, but we think that the evidence of traitorous conduct can be brought near enough to form a basis for a healthy public abhorrence of certain individuals.

The Liberal Committee of Latchford Ward is said to have passed a resolution to support Mr. Wilson when Mr. Dodds entered the field. Why? Because as a Committee it allowed itself to be dictated to by men whose unworthy aim should have been apparent to the densest understanding. Here is a clear indication of the inherent rotten- ness of the Liberal party machine. It is rotten, and there is no disguising the fact. The plain duty of the Executive of the Association is to deprive this Ward Committee of any authority to represent Latchford Liberalism any longer. As a party we need men in office who will place their convictions on a higher level than the traitors of Latchford. But their perfidy as a committee is not the worst which is to be recorded in this election. The Liberal Association has been made use of and bdsely betrayed in an even more obnoxious form. A circular and placard were issued on the eve of the poll signed apparently, at first glance, by " The Executive Committee of the Warrington Liberal Associa- tion." Only on close inspection—of the placard ' particularly—conld it be seen that the words " a member of," in small type, preceded the ostensible , official siguature. The publication was a paltry and unscrupulous attempt to mislead the electors. Wnat did it say That Alderman Pierpoint had asked the electors to vote for Mr. Dodds "as the Liberal candidate," So far the writer of the circular plainly lied. Then he went on to say "As a member of the Liberal Executive I ask you to vote for Wilson." A pretty member of the Liberal Executive, forsooth. In a couple of guesses

1

we think we could hit on the identity of this mean and cowardly Liberal (?), and probably other people have a good idea as to who he is also. The good name of•the Executive has been abominably mks- used. There is only one course open to it. As men of honour, the members are bound to disavow their anonymous colleague, and if they can by any means establish his identity they are equally bound to kick him out of the party,

There was another circular issued at the same time. If possible it left an even worse taste in the mouth than the one we have just dealt with. The signature it bore was that of " Luke Ellison "—a Liberal who once possessed an envied reputation for straightforwardness and sincerity in public life.Mr Ellison has trodden some tortuous political paths in recent years,but has done nothing more remark - able than stand sponsor to the circular in question. It teems with biased and mendacious statements, some of which are so absurd as to provoke a smile on reading them. ale sentence charges thu Non- Traders with having driven some of the " most worthy, honest, and honourable members " out of the Council. We are tempted to say: Names, please ? Why have these " honest and honour- able " members neon driven out of the Council ? Possibly, because every man has his own idea of honour, and frequently the public has another and different conception of it. All " honest and honourable " men should be able to stand their ground in the face of hostile criticism. Those Mr. Ellison's circular refers to must be of a less sturdy kind. It hardly becomes a . God-fearing man of Mr. -Ellison's type, though he may occas- sionally forget hirteetotal principles and support a brewer, to speak in another sentence of " envy, hatred, malice and all uncharitableness, from which Good Lord deliver us." Yet such lament- able expressions appear above his name. Our good friend has evidently gone to the bad politically and can now only be relied upon to : practise the opposite to what he has preached for a long lifetime. One thing we can congratulate him upon, and that is the sudden and convenient augmentation of his vocabulary for electionesring purposes. Scores of people would never have believed that Luke Ellison had written such a wonderful document had not his name neon appended to it. But, of course, seeing is believing I

There was a close and interest- SOME OTIIER WARDS. ing struggle for supremacy fa Town Hall Ward between Councillor Lord and Mr. Arthur Browne, and a gratifying success for the retiring member is to be recorded. Mr. Lord has been 'a very thorough and capable councillor, and his 'defeat would have been a real loss, particularly to the Non-Traders, in whose ranks he is one of the stalwarts. To some extent Councillor Lord's return is a justification of the publicity which he directed to the Birtles' property purchase. Whatever the merits or demerits of that transaction, the public were entitled to know more about the matter than had leaked through the some- what congested official channels. Mr. Browne would have been a stronger candidate had he not been some- what closely associated with one of the parties to the Birtles transaction. He is to be sympathised with seeing that in a previous candidature he was only beaten by a casting vote, and on Thursday the margin against him was only 45. Bewsey Ward affords another illustration of the way the official Liberals are mismanaging municipal elections. Mr. Owen Toole was entitled to the post of Liberal nominee in Bewsey Ward through having previously fought the party battle, and the substitu- tion of Mr. Morton has proved disastrous. Mr. Toole came out as an Independent and secured the seat by a big majority. We have no hesitation in congratulat- ing him, and we believe he will make a useful repre- sentative.' At the same time we are sorry for Mr. Morton, who must have felt himself in a somewhat anomalous position. He is a true Progressive and may yet find a seat under happier auspices. In St. Austin's Ward, while we are glad that the Non-Traders have retained the seat, we regret Mr. Halliwell's defeat. He fought a good fight on honest, straightforward lines, and would have made an excellent Liberal representative. The fact that he has Non-Trading sympathies makes his failure to enter the Council all the more regrettable. If he had avowed his views he might have stood a better chance of snatching the seat from his Convervative opponent. The three-cornered fight in Orford Ward provided plenty of ground for speculation prior to the poll, and there were many who doubted Councillor Cunning- ton's ability to hold the seat for the Non-Traders. He has, however, succEieded by a substantial majority. Them were some close municipal contests at War- rington, but none came up to that in Waterloo Ward, Widnes, where an independent candidate obtained a majority of 9in a three-cornered fight. At least one successful candidate at Warrington municipal elections took the result for granted. On the reading of the figures for Howley Ward, Coun- cillor Sankey produced a sheet of paper and read a brief but carefully prepared' speech to the cheering multitude which congregated on the lawn. Many people will consider it tin-Worthy conduct! * * There is trouble in the Tory camp. Mr. W. M. Sloane, who is apparently not taking his defeat in Orford Ward philosophically,rounded on certain party leaders at the after-meeting at the Junior Conservative Club for having " neglected " him. We can quite understand his feelings, for the party activity seems to have been almost entirely concentrated on the Latchford and St. John's Wards. * * * It was amusing at the Council meeting on Tuesday to hear the various ways of pronouncing " Carnegie Apparently every speaker had a different version, and each one trotted it out with the utmost confidence. We will endeavour to render" Carnegie" as it was spoken There was Carniggi, Carneggie, Karnegie, Carnejie, and others impossible to recall. Perhaps the Library Committee will enquire from the millionaire how lie pronounces his apparently simple name himself. * * * There is a rather musty flavour about some pro- ceedings which have just been taken in the Court of Appeal at Dublin. As far back as 1707 Robert Smith a porter merchant, was declared a bankrupt, and the case is not out of Courts yet. In 1800 a composition of 2s. in the pound was paid. A hundred years later property belonging to the bankrupt has been dis- covered which allows of 20s. in the pound being paid to the representatives of the eighteenth-century creditors.

* * Book Canvasser : " Here I have an excellent,' volume on swimming. It tells you how to begin, howe to become an expert, and how to win competitions." " Yes ; but does it say anything about drowning ?" ' asked the nervous young man. Most certainly, my dear sir. It gives full details. When you feel like drowning you have only to read carefully page one hundred and six, and you'll see bust how to save yourself."—" Daily New:." * An amusing instance of the rewards awaiting those who devote themselves to the public service was given at Pwllbeli Town Council on Tuesday night. The Mayor and others expressed regret at the retire- ment of Mr. Saddler Jones from the Council. Mr. Saddler Jones said he had done his best for the town during eight and a half years and his only recom- pense had been to be called either a demon, an old devil, or an old fool -a remark which was naturally received with loud laughter. There is hope for the tram-track when two respon- sible Committees have settled their differences. A contract for the re-laying of the setts has been let. Meanwhile vehicle-users will be interested in the opinion of Alderman Smethurst (Chairman of the Tramways Committee) that the track is as good as any in Lancashire. The Alderman ought to be a enlist and not a theorist and then he would know something.

It will interest our readers to learn that to-morrow is the 30th anniversary of the Rev. John Yonge as minister of Wycliffe Church. Seeing that his services have not been confined to his own Church but have .extended to all the Free Churches in the town and district it would be a graceful and merited oomph.. meat to the reverend gentleman if themembers of these churches were to gather in large numbers at Wycliffe to-morrow, and thus show their appreciation of the veteran Congregationalist's Christian work in the town over so long a peri.od*..J., Some curious incidents at the cemetery have been related by Alderman Evansit seems that the body of a pauper found in the country district of Hollins Green was taken to the Warrington cemetery by an under- taker and placed in the mortuary. No one else knew anything of the matter till the body was subsequently discovered there. Its condition was so bad that a hasty burial was imperative. Then the rumour got abroad that there had been no religious ceremony and more trouble arose. Fortunately the Cemetery authorities were able to deny this, and the matter ended with the reproof of the undertaker for taking liberties with the Corporation mortuary. * * * The " Who Told "inquiry committee came in for some criticism at the Council meeting,and no wonder, far their report could not have been much balder. Councillor Wilkinson obliged with what Councillor Craik termed " a dramatic recital" of the ways and means the Committee had adopted to find out the wrong-doer. He even went so far as to give a sort of half assurance that the information had not come from an official of the Corporation. The scent seems to have been getting warmor just when the Committee ceased its deliberations, and it is a pity the inquiry could not have been narrowed down a little more. A voucher of good conduct might shortly have been forthcoming on behalf the Aldermen and Councillors. As it is there are thirty-six of them trembling under a dark suspicion.

Lovers of old customs lament the disappearance of

the old customs associated with All Hallows' Eve, bat it is forgotten that two of the chief pieces of ritual,the lighting of fires and the eating of cakes, have been transferred from October 31st to the sth of November. ,Loug before the Gunpowder Plot affair bonfires were 'ighted on the hilltops of Ireland and Scotland. Sir John Sinclair in his Statistical Account of Scotland, Published in 1793, mentioned that in the Highlands bonfires used to be lighted and consecrated cakes baked on the Ist. /November (the first day of winter), and also on the Ist of May (the first day of spring). In North Wales the autumnal fire was called Coel Coeth; it was accompanied by such ceremonies as leaping through the fire (as on St. John's Eve in Germany and other Countries), throwing nuts in the fire, and biting k at apples suspended from a string. One sometimes . sees people leap across a half-consumed bonfire on the sth of November, saying in excuse that it was an old custom. - ,

o o At the Women's Suffrage meeting the Rev. J. F. Matthews went rather further than most of those Present in support of the "suffragettes." He would, lie said, "dearly like to see Miss Pankhurst on the floor of the House of Commons dressing down the weak-kneed Liberals and the long- faced Tories." We wonder how the dressing- down process would act upon the progress of legislative measures. Would the ladies, if they got into the House, ever obey the ruling of the Speaker ? 'Judging by their present defiance of the law and all Its works there would have to be more carryings out as a sequal to the inevitable carryings on. The new local branch of the Women's Suffrage League proposes to agitate for reform on more orthodox lines. They have a good cause, and if they are content to hasten slowly, solid progress towards placing women on an equal footing with men in regard to the franchise should be assured. Responsible, ratepaying women ought, most certainly to have the vote, and they will get it all the sooner by showing a quiet determina- tion and a capacity for restraint as well as enthusiastic demonstration.

* * According to an estimate made a few years ago something like £BO,OOO is expended on the evening of 'November 5, at an average cost of £266 a minute, on firewogiks alone in this country. The sum expended on bo/fires cannot be estimated owing to the lack of -trustworthy data, but another little item makes a respectable total. The two million paper masks annually donned by small revellers cost between £16,000 and £17,000. The largest and costliest annual bonfires in the world are those erected on two hills outside Salem, Massachusetts, at the American fireworks festival, the Fourth of July (Independence Day), when two giant pyramids, includ- ing 4,000 barrels each, are burned. The most elaborate Fourth of July bonfires, however, are those which are lighted on Pike's Peak, Colorado, and Mount Pisgah, at Cripple Creek, on the same clay. The Peak is 14,147 feet in height, and on the stroke of midnight a pile which includes 1,7001 b. of red, white, and blue powder—the national colours—is set on fire by electricity. The flames shoot upwards for hundreds of feet, and make the snow-capped crest of 'the mountain look like an active volcano.

PARISH GHURCH SALE OF WORK.

-- Proposed Church Institute.

I The annual sale ofiwork in connection with the War- rington Parish Church commenced in the Fairfield Schools on Thursday. The special object which it is desired to promote this year is the provision of a Church Institute for the parish, and it is to be hoped that the results of the efforts will be such that the work can be commenced. The product of many months of self- denying labour was shown on the stalls,.-and if the enthusiasm of the promoters of the sale is met with a fitting response from the general public, its success is assured. The stall-holders are :—Parochial 'stall : Mesdames Craik, S. Taylor' J. Fairclough Antwis, Lees and Pharoe and Miss Shaw. Mrs. Willis' stall: Mesdames Willis. Rylands, P. Rylands and John Davies, and the Misses Litton and L. White. G.F.S. stall: The Misses White and Associates of the Society. `. Men's Bible Class stall: Mesdames Brandreth, Burrows, Hill, Tilling, Fox and Carmen, and the Misses Hopkins, James and Brandreth. St. George's Mission . stall: Mrs. Shaw, Mrs. Barratt, Mrs. Heatherington, and the Misses Buckley, Bowles and Shaw. St. Clements Mission Stall : Mesdames Barker, Hall, Grey, Sanders, and Collins, and the Misses I. Hicks,and H. Hicks. Flower Stall : The Misses Sankey, Evans and Bishop. Children's Stall : Misses Collins and Battomley, Fish Pond :—Misses Stockton and Wads- worth. Pincushion Stall : Miss Stevenson. Refresh- ment Stall : Mrs. William Fairclough and the Misses Willis, Wilcocks, Brookes, F. Price, Rogers, Woods and E. Taylor. Thursday's opening ceremony should have been per- formed by Mrs. Hall Walker, but owing to a prior engagement she was unable to attend, her place being taken by Mr. Henry Greenall. The RECTOR, who presided over a large attendance, explained that it was desired to construct a Church Institute in a central position which men could use at a moderate charge. It was also desired to wipe off the debt for the extension of St. George's Mission. On this they had spent £1,200 out of which they still had about £7OO to raise. (Hear, hear.) Mrs. GREEXLL gracefully performed the office allotted to her. She expressed her hearty concurrence with the objects of the sale. An institute was a great help not only to the people of the parish but to the clergy. She hoped friends from the outside districts would rally round and give their support to the sale. '-(Applause). On the motion of Councillor CRAIK, seconded by Mr. D &REIN', Mrs. Greenall was heartily thanked for her ser- vices. In the evening a very enjoyable concert was given, items being contributed by the St. Georges Mission choir and male voice quartette party, Mr. and Mrs. Pfannen, Mr. Sumner, and Mr. Whittle. Thursday's sales realized £lOO. The sale was continued yesterday/Mrs. J. D. Buckton Performing the opening ceremony. There WilS again a large attendance.

WARRINGTON.

Coming Events. The Council of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federa- tion of Juuior Liberals will attend the weekly meeting of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association on Monday. The subject under discussion will be Ruskin's "Social Economy." A concert in aid of the funds of the Warrington and District Nursing Association will be held in the Parr Hall on November 29th. An excellent pro- gramme has been arranged and there is every prospect of the venture being a great success. Particulars as to prices ac., will be found in our advertising columns. The artistes will include Miss Evangeline Florence, England's greatest ballad concert soloist, and Miss E. Yunson,the renowned Scotch contralto. The Warrington Musical Society open their concert season on Wednesday in the Parr Hall. The pro- gramme will be an unusually attractive one and will include Gades " Crusaders" and Stanford's " Songs of the Sea." The artistes will be Miss Edith Clegg, mezzo-soprano, Mr. James Davies, tenor, and Mr. Frederick Austin, baritone. For prices of admission &c., we refer our readers to page 1. The series of meetings for men in Toft's Assembly room will be continued on Thursdey, when the Rev. J. F. Matthews will speak on " Religion and Social Life." A mass meeting of Churchpeople of Warrington will be held in the Parr Hall on Tuesday evening, when speeches on the Education Bill will be delivered. Mr. J. C. Parr will preside, and addresses will be given by the Yen. Archdeacon Madden and Hr. lan Malcolm, ! who for many years was a distinguished member of diplomatic service. , On Tuesday evening the Rev. Arthur Mursell, of London, will deliver his popular lecture on " 50 years of public lecturing both sides of the Channel, the Border and the Atlantic" in the Bold-street schools. Mr. James Dodds will preside. New Valuer Appointed. ! At a meeting of the Warrington Assessment Com- mittee yesterday, Mr. Meacock of Leigh, was appointed valuer in succession to Mr. William Owen who has resigned. Personal. Among the King's Counsel recently appointed is Mr. A. J. Ashton, a native of Warrington, son of the late Mr. Walter Ashton, of the Manchester and Liver- pool District Bank. Mr. Ashton received his early education at the Boteler Grammar School, and subse- quentiv at the Manchester Grammar School. He ! proceeded to Oxford, where he was a scholar of Baliol College and took first-class honours. Mr. Ashton is a ! member of the Northern Circuit. Warrington was well represented at the Liverpool ! Diocesan Conference on Tuesday and speeches on the question of Church discipline were made by the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), and the Rev. E. C. E Carleton. We regret to learn that Mr. George Thompson, the representative of Great Sankey on the Warrington !Board of Guardians and Rural District Council, has for the some time been seriously ill. It is, however, ' pleasing to know that he is now progressing satisfac- torily and has gone to Colwyn Bay in the hope that he will be greatly benefitted by the change of air. Sympathetic references to Mr. Thompson's illness were made at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday and the members heartily endorsed the hope expressed by the Chairman that he would soon be restored to health and be able to resume his public duties. Alderman S. Quinn (Mayor) presided over a special meeting of the Widnes Council, held in the Town Hall, on Tuesday afternoon. Alderman Gossage, in referring to the minutes of the Health Committee' made a strong plea in respect of workmen residing out of the town , being treated at the Accident Hospital free of charge. They were employed by Widnes men, and they ought to have the priviledge of going into the hospital. The hospital was built for alleviating the suffering of those who met with accidents in Widnes.— Several members ' strongly resented this suggestion, and said that the hospital was supported by the rates of the town, and it ' I was only fair to the ratepayers that it should be kept for their express purpose. The remaining portion of the minutes called for no comment.— At the conclusion of the business, Mr. Lewis moved the following notice of motion :—" That this Council, whilst recognising the responsibility of the police , authorities to preserve the thoroughfares of the tow.). I free from annoyance and obstruction, appeals to them ' to refrain from a sweeping and indiscriminare prohibi- I tion of the use of Victoria-square for the purpose of public assembly, and that copies of this resolution be I sent to the Chief Constable of the county and Mr. I Superintendent Strickland."—The Mayor remarked that they were aware that at the last Council meeting in which a similar notice of motion had been made, he had I given the matter his careful consideration and ruled it out of order. His opinion had not changed in the least, as it was not policy on their part to interfere with the police; but, wlth a view of getting an expression of opinion from the remaining members of the Council, Ihe would allow Mr. Lewis to proceed.—Mr. Lewis's I oration in support of the motion did not convince more I than a seconder, and it was lost,—Another notice of ' motion—" That it is undesirable to continue the exist- ing arrangement with the county for the policing of the borough, and that the General Purposes Committee be instructed to take such steps as are necessary for , the institution of a borough force "—owing to the late hour, was held over until the next Council meeting.

SEQUEL TO A WIDNES FIRE. SALESMAN CHARGED WITH ARSON.

At Widnes, on Monday, John Joseph Marsh, a young; salesman in the employ of Messrs. W. A. Golsby and ; Ca., book factors, of Seacombe,was charged with malici- ously setting fire to a boot shop in Wickes-road on ; September 23. Prisoner pleaded not guilty. Mr. ; Golsby said that he carried on business as Golbsy and Co. He occupied the shop 71, Widnes-road. Prisoner had been in his employ since November. Some time in July prisoner took charge of his shop in Widnes road and remained in his employ until September 5. There was a deficiency in his books of 12s. 11d, at that time, and a few days later Marsh sent him a telegram stating that the shop had been on dre. On going to Widnes he found the shop damaged to the extent of £lll 15s. He asked Marsh how it happened, and he replied that he closed the shop between 7.30 and took a pair of leggings to the boot shop opposite, and on coming out he saw a crowd round Golsby's shop. When he got there he found it on fire. He burst open the door, and tried to save the books. Witness commenced a search round the premises, but could see no cause for the outbreak. The circumstances were reported to the insurance company the next day. The assessors came to examine the cause and arrive at the extent of the damage. A deficiency of £l3 was afterwards found. The prisoner received £1 per week. On September 30 he said to witness that he wanted to get the thing off his mind, and confessed that it was he that had set the shop on fire. Information was given to the police.—ln cross-examination, prisoner contended that the boots were stolen on the night of the fire, which was accountable for the deficiency.—Violet Thompson, residing at 2, Dean-street, Widnes, said she was assistant ; saleswoman, and looked after the cash. She took the cash home with her every night, and handed it to Sir. Golsby when he came to Widnes. —Police-Constable Amer stated that he was on the scene of the fire a few rainutes after it broke out. Witness and Marsh forced open the door, and Marsh rushed out with the books.—Superintendent Strickland ; said that the prisoner was brought to him, when he said," I struck a match, lighted a cigarette, and threw ; the match down, and that is how the shop must have ; caught fire." When the usual caution had been read I out to him by the magistrate, prisoner said, "I strongly ; deny that I made that statement of having set fire to the shop."—He was committed to the next Liverpool ;Assizes, the bench allowing the bail, himself in £lO and ; two sureties of £lO.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 3, 1906.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

COMPENSATION CLAIM AT RUNCORN. On Monday, at Runcorn County Court, before his Honour Judge Shires Will, Richard Littler,l2l, Surrey- street, brought an action action against the Salt Union, Limited, Weston Point for compensation. Littler was engaged in discharging coal from flats, and on January 2nd of last year, while the applicant was moving a truck by means of a crowbar, the latter slipped and caused him to fall, striking the bar with the right side of the chest. The lung was injured, and it was contended that the applicant was incapicitated from being able to work in future. —The case was adjourned until November 13th.

WIDNES MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

(6 Only two wards of the town provided any election- eering—in Victoria Ward, where Mr. W. Moss (Labour) was opposing the retiring member, Mr. Pierce Tallon, and in Waterloo Ward, where there was far more interest because of the three-cornered fight. Coun- cillor Charles Hemingway retired altogether, and the aspirants for the vacant seat were Mr. Dan Brennan (Independent), Mr. John Milier (I.L.P. and Free Church Council), and Mr. A. G. Wright, the nominee of the Ward Committee. The day was a miserable one for polling, and only moderate business was done up to tea- time. Heavy polls were not expected. The results were declared soon after eight o'clock as follow : Victoria Ward : Tallon, 461 ; Moss, 196 ; majority 265. Waterloo Ward : Brennan, 203 ; Wright, 194 ; Miller, 113 ; majority, 9. ST HELENS.

ST. HELENS EDUCATION AUTHORITY AND CONTRACrs.

' Colonal W. W. Pilkington presided at the final meet- ing of the St. Helens Education Committee on Wednes.- day, hold at the Town Hall.—Hr. Forshaw proposed that the following clause be inserted in all contracts made by the committee :—" The contractor shall pay his workmen not less than the minimum trades union rate of wages in the district for each class of labour respectively, and shall observe the recognised hours and conditions of labour generally."—Mr. Hamblett seconded.—Alderman Forster said the resolution would have been a great deal better if it had been passed as sent up by the Parliamentary Co rumittc3, with the words minimum standard rate of wages " instead of "trades union rates."—The resolution was carried with- out opposition. ST. HELENS TOWN COUNCIL. The Mayor (Mr. H. Martin) presided on \ Vednesday at the final meeting of the St. Helens Town Council. —Mr. Rudd referred to a resolution which stated that Messrs. John Swallow and Co. had asked the Corpora- tion, in the event of a new engineering works being erected by the company in the Sutton district within the bo,ough, to assist them to the extent of allowing them to be exempt from rates to the end of the year 1910. The committee had decided not to grant the application. He (Mr. Rudd) asked if it was not the custom in other towns to encourage the manufacturers to settle in a town by relieving them of rates for the first two or three years.—Alderman J. C. Gamble said they had no power to do anything of the sort.—The minutes were adopted. ST. HELENS MAN DIVORCED.

In the Divorce Court, on Monday afternoon, before the President, Sir Gorell Barnes, a case was heard in which Mrs. Elizabeth Linford, living at Raunds, Northamptonshire9 sued for a dissolution of her marriage with Mr. John Smart Linford, living in Bronte-street, St. Helens, formerly of Brackley and Bedlord, a farmer and dealer, against whom she alleged misconduct and desertion.—The case was undefended.— Mr. Metcalf appeared for the petitioner, who gave evidence that she was married to the respondent on April 22, 1891, at Raunds. He was at the tima a farmer and dealer. They lived at Raunds, Brackley, and Bed- ford, where he kApt the Plough Hotel for a time. He gave way to drink and left her, and went to St. Helens. Since then he had not supported her. She did not see him after 1901, when he came to Bedford on a visit. She eventually discovered that he and a woman were living together in St. Helens as Mr. and Mrs. Smart.— Other evidence was given in support, and a decree nisi was granted. ST. HELENS HOOLIGAN PUNISHED.

Joseph M`Naughton was charged on remand at St. Helens on Monday with an aggravated assault on Mrs. Sarah Woodward, of Grange Park-road, and also with a common assault on Charles Wood, collier, of 238, Boundary-road.—The Chief Constable said the case had been reduced to one of wounding, because it was doubt- ful if Mrs. Woodward would be able to attend at the assizes.—Charles Wood said that on Saturday night, October 13th, at half-past nine, he was in Tontine- street, when the prisoner came to him and asked him to have a drink. Wood declined, and prisoner went away up the street. He then came dashing down the street and struck Wood in the jaw. Continuing his career he met Mrs. Woodward and her husband. He struck Mrs. Woodward full in the nose, knocking her down.—Dr. Mason said Mrs. Woodward had suffered from concus- sion of the brain, and was for some time in a dangerous condition.—The Chairman said it was a most cowardly and unprovoked assault, and they would take care that prisoner had no chance of of doing anything of the same kind for some time. He must go to gaol for six months for the assault on Mrs. Woodward, end he would be fined ss. and costs, or fourteen days, for the assault on Wood.
