OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

All Saints' Mission.

The harvest festival services in connection with the above mission were continued on Sunday, when ths preacher, morning and evening, was Mr. Goss, of Leeds, formerly of Warrington. Throughout the day there were large congregations, particularly in the evening, when many were unable to obtain admission. An 'neon- lent rendering was given of the Magnincat to music by Caleb Simper, and the anthem, which was equally well sung, was "How manifold are Thy works." Mr. Utley presided at the organ. The room had been tastefully decorated by Miss Westwell, Mrs. Baker, Mrs. Perks and Miss Kenwright.

Wycliffe P.S.A. The weekly meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society was held on Sunday, There was a very fair attendance considering the inclemency of the weather. Mr. George ' Wallington presided, and spoke of the consolation and the good derived from the singing of hymns. He (the speaker) had received inspiration mady times from the words and the singing of favourite hymns. He hoped all would gain the same inspiration as the need arose. Mr. Fookes, of Manchester, took for his subject "Duty." He spoke of the great painting now being exhibited at the Free Trade Hall, Manchester, entitled "Despised and rejected." He said, as he saw from the picture, how the world was following in the line of sin and pleasure without even a thought of Jesus, the despised and rejected. He urged all men to think. If they would do so, it would show them the path of duty, in the highest *ease. Two solos were rendered in a very pleasing manner by Miss Armstrong. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The Rev. J. Yonge and Mr. Joseph Garner also took part in the meeting.—A vote of condolence was passed with Mrs. Rowbothara in the loss, by death, of her husband, ex-Inspector Rowbotham of the Borough Police. He was a member of this brotherhood until quite recently. The Rev. J. Yonge spoke of him as an upright and honest man, and I a man who was liked by all who know him.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Mary's Church, Buttermarket-street. The New Bells.

The bells have now been hunr, in the new tower of St. Mary's Church, Buttermarket-street, and on Sunday they were rung for the first time. They are manipu- lated by the Ellacombe chiming apparatus' and a very pleasing and melodious effect is produced, the tone of the bell; being much admired. In commemoration of the event special services were held. The Bishop of Liverpool celebrated pontifical high mass at eleven and a sermon was preached by the Rev. J. Jagger, S.J., on the subject " God's callings." Gounod's Mass No. 5 for male voices was effectively sung by members of St. Mary's, St. Alban's, St. Mary's, Latchford, and Church of the Sacred Heart choirs. The offertory piece was "Ave Maria" (Franz Abt). Mr. Alban Boyle conducted and Mr. J. Eckersley presided at the organ. In the evening, when solemn vespers were sung according to the Benedictine rite, the sermon was preached by the Right Rev. Abbot Gasquett, D.D., 0.5.8., on the sub- ject : "The Catholic Church in the Past and Present." There was a large congregation. The rev, gentleman preached from the text : " Thou, 0 God, art ever the self-same, and Thy years shall not fail. The children of Thy servants shall continue, and their seed shall be maintained for ever." Tne preacher asked his hearers to look back across the centuries into the records of the past in this country, and said that if they did so one thing about the people in the latter meclixval days I stood out before almost everything else ; and that was their love for their religion and for their churches which were scattered in such numbers all over the length and breadth of the country. The preacher then in a most interesting way dealt with local ecclesiastical history of the period, alluding to the foundation of chantries in Warrington and neighbourhood, and specially mentioning the one given to the Parish Church, through the munificence of Sir Thomas Boteler. There could be little doubt that in Warrington, as everywhere else, the people vied with one another in helping on every church work, and according to their means contributing to beautify the sanctuary where God dwelt in their midst. Where they could not give riches they gave in kind,and many were the rings and gowns and bed-hangings 90 doubt left to yonder old parish church in Catholic days to adorn the rtatutes of the Blessed Mother and saints that stood there ; in fact it was hardly a stretch of imagination to think that the old vestment discovered years ago walled up in an old staircase of the Warrington Church was one of those same vestments hidden away in 1559 when the old office of the mass was proscribed, and copes, vestments and altar cloths were hastily got rid of, and the altars that then stood in the Parish Church razed to the ground. Reference was made to the change of religion in the 16th centuary—a "sad and dismal subject' hardly fitted for a day of rejoic- ing like that—and to the fact that the religion and religious teaching of England which hadl been in existence for ten centuries was changed, at the will of the King and his ' officials. The Catholic spirit of the people asserted itself, and England produced those heroic bands of men and women who all over the country resisted the might of the State, and remained staunch to their religious principles in spite of every penalty and punishment which the ingenuity of officials could devise. Towards the close of the 18th century there was a slight improvement and a slight toleration. In 1770 a Benedictine, Father Thomas B. Shuttleworth, who had spent nearly .10 years in ministering to the poor Catholics. of Woolston, moved to Warrington, and began regular Catholic services. He died, and was succeeded by Father Bradshaw, who, in 1778, built the first chapel, which served until in 1813 Dr. Molynenx erected the old chapel of St. Albans in Bewsey-street. The rest was known to them, and, said the preacher in conclusion, as he looked round about that day, and saw the walls of that sanctuary, with its arches and pillars, and spring roof, and as he cast his eves upon the altars which had been raised to God and Ills saints, he seemed to understand their feelings of joy that day.

THE ELECTIONS. SOME SURPRISING RESULTS. Non-Traders Hold Their Seats.

With contests in seven out of the nine wards of the borough, more than usual interest centred in the municipal elections which took place on Thursday. Some surprising results were yielded. Mr. James Dodds, the champion of the Non-Trading movement, suffered a heavy defeat at the hands of Ir. W. J. Wilson in Latchford Ward where the contest was one of the keenest ever witnessed. Mr. T.S. Steel succeeded in wresting the repreSentation of St. John's Ward from Mr. John • Magirm by a great majority, and Mr. Owen Toole easly accounted for Mr. J. A. J.Morton in Bewsey Ward. In the other Wards the results were mostly what had been expected—Messrs. J. Lord (Town Hall) E. Cunning,ton (Orford), J. Sankey (Howley), and T. H. Ashcroft (St. Austin's), retaining their seats in the Council.

The fact that the Non-Trading question was again a great issue in at least two wards, lent additional importance to the verdict of the electorate. The work of electioneering had been prosecuted with much vigour by the majority of the candidates, and the voters had no excuse for apathy. There were meet- ings galore, while some districts were inundated with literature,and a lively warfare was carried on through the medium of the hoardings. There was every indication that the electors entered into the spirit of the contests and showed that they were not content to let things go by default. The introduction of the political element into muni- cipal contests has been strongly deprecated by the Non-Traders. In a poster which attracted general attention and was headed " Paltry politics and Tammany tactics in good old Warrington " they alleged the existence of a compact between the Con- servative and Liberal parties to defeat the Non-Traders. This called forth a spirited reply from Mr. IV. Peter Rylands, chairman of the Conservative Association, who affirmed that the efforts of the Non-Traders were being specially directed against the Conservative party. In order to disprove this contention, the Non-Traders issued, a second poster showing they had acted on independent lines in all the wards. All eyes were turned towards Latchford Ward where the contest was vigorously fought on the Non- Trading question. Mr. Edward Walker had been selected by the Non-Trading Association to champion their cause against the retiring member (Councillor Wilson) but ill-health prevented him entering the fray. Mr. James Dodds, the leader of the movement. yielded to pressure to stand but he had to contend with unusually severe opposition. The chief issue on I which he sought the support of the ratepayers was the acceptance by Councillor Wilson of £3O for his services as Mayor's auditor. The Non-Traders con- tended that this course was both illegal and highly , improper, in face of the declaration in the Municipal Corporations Acts that no Councillor should derive lany profit from his position as a representative of the , ratepayers. On the other hand, the supporters of Mr. Wilson quoted the opinion of the standing counsel of the Municipal Corporations Association who stated.:—" Section 246 of the Public Health Act of 175 authorizes the payment of the Mayor's Auditor, I and he is appointed by the Mayor, see section 21, sub-section, of the Municipal Corporation Act, 1882, and not by the Town Council, he does not become disqualified under section 12." Mr.Dodds, in address- ing the constituency, emphasised the danger involved in the acceptance of the principle of payment to a ' Mayor's auditor. "It was," he said " the thin end of the wedge " and "if some of those who formerly had, say 30 times £3O, realized that Mr. Wilson had had £3O without being pulled up, they would soon be I back and at their old game, and if Mr. Wilson should raise objection, what weight would his objection have? None whatever. He would be told he had had his hit, and he had better let others have theirs, or they , would stop his £30." Mr. Dodds also asked the , burgesses to approve the Non-Trading cause as it had had the effect of stopping several mad schemes of capital expenditure, and at a successful public meet- ing which he held in the ward on Tuesday evening ihe had the strong support of Alderman Pierpoint and I the Rev. T. F. Matthews. Considerable feeling had been imported into the contest by certain parties. A circular was issued purporting to be signed by a member of the Executive Committee of the Warring- ton Liberal Association " and calling upon the burgesses to vote for Wilson (who, of course, was the nominee of the Conservative party) and" so help to put an end to the campaign of scurrility and abuse which has disgraced the town and damaged your trade for the past five years." Another appeal on behalf of the Conservative candi- date was issued in the name of Councillor Luke Ellison, the Liberal member for the ward, and des- cribed the Non-Trading movement as " the greatest autocratic balderdash ever introduced into the town" asserting that "the time has now come when we should sink all party differences and drive this tyranny out of our ward for ever." Mr. Dodds in a final word to the electorate, reminded them of the solemn I responsibility which rested upon them and urged them to back up the cause of municipal purity which he , has so zealously championed. I There was a battle royal in St. John's Ward where the return of Councillor Maginn was challenged by /kfr. Thomas S. Steel, the nominee of the Conservative Association. The retiring representative had an excel- lent record of work on independent lines and had the support of such prominent Conservatives as Mr. L. Greening, Alderman Forshaw,and Council lorDr. Joseph. The campaign was waged with the greatest enthusiasm, meetings on both sides having been held almost every evening for a week. The electors were entertained with quite a lively duel between the candidates, Mr. Steel twitting Councillor Magian on the fact that he had in the past given huge picnics to children and alleging that these were in some measure responsible for his return on the last occasion. Councillor Magian retorted that he would have given more treats to the little ones, had his pocket been deeper, and made capital out of Mr. Steel's attendance at St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church on Sunday, stating that: "He believed Mr. Steel was born in one faith and reared in another; he became a sidesman in a Church of England church in that locality; and he was bound to he all right now for he turned Catholic on Sunday and attended High Mass. What did they think of a man who used those means of seeming their votes?" Mr. Steel made a great point of the religious education question, while his opponent appeared to consider it outside the province of a municipal contest and only touched upon educational matters by arguing for the abolition of school fees. In Town Hall Ward there was a particularly close con- test,Councillor Lord having a formidable opponent in Mr. Arthur Browne who hi his fight against the Non-Trader had the support of prominent Conservatives and, he stated in his address, of " the clergy of all denomina- tions." The question of the Birtles property purchase which Cotaicillor Lord has so strongly criticised figured largely in this fight.. While Mr. Brown urged the rate- payers to express their disappro‘al of the continual " running down of the town." Councillor Lord asked them to " vote for the man who has no family axe to grind." Although there were no meetings, both candi- dates worked hard as was evidenced by the exceedingly close result.

--In-He-ws—e-y- Ward Mr. John A. J. Morton was the official Liberal candidate and be was backed up by the Progressim Labour League of which he is the'secretary. His opponent was Mr. Owen Toole, who has previously contested the seat unsuccessfully and who came out as an Independent. Mr. Morton laid par- ticular stress on questions closely affecting the workers, and pointed out that he had made a special study of the unemployed problem. He believed it was a question of wages chiefly. If they wished to deal effectually with the housing question they passed laws which enforced a csrtain standard of houses, but the chief element in attaining that was that they should provide a means of obtaining the wages necessary to pay the rent. That must necessarily bo the basis—the raising of the wages of the working classes so that they might be enabled to pay for and attain what they desired. He also declared himself an advocate of municipal purity. Mr. Toole appealed to the Ward on the education qitestion, and said he would make it his special duty to protect the rights of parents. At a public meeting on' his behalf the speakers included Mr. L. Greening and Mr. W. Madden, the latter of whom contended that in common gratitude for the services :rendered during the last Parliamentary election Mr. Toole should have been treated differently by the Liberal party. There was a three-cornered fight in Orford Ward,both the Socialists and the Conservatives making strenuous

Mr. W. rook championed the interests of the Independent Labour Party and conducted a very vigorous campaign, while ; Mr. W. McNeil Sloane was the Conservative candidate. The Non-Traders and the Socialists held meetings in the ward, while the Conservatives relied upon can- vassing as a means of reaching the electorate. It was taken for granted that Councillor Sankey (C) would retain the representation of Howley Ward, his independent opponent, Mr. Walter Worthy, only enter- ing the field at the last moment. Interest in the contest was not very great and no meetings were held.

ELECTION DAY.

! Thursday was a typical November election day—dull and raw, with occasional rain—and electioneering under such conditions was not altogether cheerful. The 1 workers, however, threw themselves with surprising energy into the task of bringing voters to the poll, and numerous motor cars and other vehicles were placed at the disposal of the candidates. During the dinner hour and in the evening polling was particularly brisk. After the declaration of the results, the customary votes'of thanks were accorded to the returning officers and officials.

Councillor WlLsox moved a vote of thanks to the Mayor as returning officer for Latchford Ward, and said the fight between Mr. Dodds and himself had been a very friendly one. If he had the honour to meet the same opponent again he hoped they would fight in the same friendly spirit. Mr. DODDS seconded, and said he desired to thank the Mayor for the way in which the election had been carried through. He did not fight for votes : he fought for a principle, and he was perfectly satisfied in his conscience and in every other way. The MAron acknowledged the vote. Councillor LORD moved a similar compliment to Alderman Smethurst and said be did not think he had ever seen the Alderman in a happier mood than he had been that in day. Mr. BROWSE seconded. Everything, he said, had been done very amicably and he must express his gratitude not only to Alderman Smethurst but to all the officials.

Mr. G. 11.1 V. STEEL, on behalf of his brother, moved a vote of thanks to Alderman Tinnion.

Mr. MAGLNN seconded and paid a tribute to the way in which the presiding officer and all officials had con- ducted the election. He had fought three contests and had' won one and he had found the officials just as courteous to him on this occasion as previously. No fawning or favour was shown to him even when he won by a greater majority than he had now lost by. The Mayor had held the scales of justice equally, even when the party to which he (the Mayor) belonged, influential friends of his opponent, and his opponent himself brought all the forces they possibly could to compel him to be a traitor to justice. Since he had the honour of representing the ward, he and the Mayor had had many quarrels, but he had always found the Mayor a gentleman and in spite of their disagree- ments there had not been the least animosity. On the motion of Councillor CuNNINGToN seconded by Mr. POOK a vote of thanks was accorderl to Coun- cillor Forshaw.

Similar compliments were accorded to Alderman Francomb (Howley Ward), Alderman Bennett (St' Austin's) and Alderman Evans (Bewsey).

Speeches From the Town Hall Steps. Lively Scene

A great crowd assembled on the lawn facing the Town I Hall to hear the public declaration of the results. I Cheers and counter-cheers greeted the reading of the I figures. Afterwards several of the candidates delivered I 1 speeches. I Mr. MAGINN said he wished to pay his tribute to the i inhabitants of St. John's Ward, whether they voted for him or not, and he would pay an extra tribute to those ' who returned their votes in his favour. He had not a 1 1 single word to say in disparagement of those who voted against him. If they voted in their opinion rightly, he was quite satisfied. It was useless for him to say he was altogether satisfied with the result, because if he said so he would be helloing human nature. All through one's life it was a pleasure to win and a litle hurting to lose. Mr. MAGINN proceeded to thank his workers and supporters and said he was deeply thankful to those Conservative friends who in face of the opposition of their party, in face of the most combined monopoly ever known in the district, and in face of the prejudice and sneers of their friends and relatives, were manly enough to support him. It was with sincere regret that he was forced to stand down and hand over the representation of the ward to his opponent, Mr. Steel. He specially regretted, for the sake of a small number in St. John's Ward, that he had to hand over the Catholic representation of the ward to his opponent. He would, however, ask his Catholic friends who were now represented by Mr. Steel not to take him too severely, for a convert might be easily misled, especially a convert who had travelled round the clock of religion and turned Catholic last Sunday. (Cheers and hooting). Continuing Mr. Maginn said he always thonght he had done a little for the town of his adoption—the town into which he tramped practically bare-foot and in which he had worked until he now paid the highest taxes of any local shopkeeper. He thought when he fought his first opponent that he took up that position for the sake of the town, more than for his own sake. lllif little effort in St John's Ward had certainly net been satisfactory to him nor, he believed, to his friends. But ha would point out that he had not fought individuals. He had fought the

greatest and richest and worst monopoly the town ever had.lle had fought the monopoly of that discredited body of men—the tied-house brewers. He fought them single-handed and he would fi.ght again, whether he was returned or not. He would see before he died— (the remainder of Mr. Maginn's remarks were drowned in groans and hooting). Councillor STEEL thanked the electors of St. John's Ward for having returned him by such a handsome majority. (Cheers.) He was proud to think that the workingmen had realized in him their friend and he hoped that during l the three years he represented them in the Council he would merit the confidence they had placed in him. Mr. Magian had bean playing a losing game for some time. He had been trying to say that the Roman Catohlics could not trust him (Mr. Steel) ; but the Roman Catholics had said they would rather leave their interests in his hands than in the hands of Mr. Magian. The election had shown what could be done in St. John's Ward when there were no picnics in the field. From his (Mr. Steel's) point or view, they had fought the election fairly and squarely,, and they had come out on top. (Cheers). He hoped , that in the Town Council he would be able to do seine- thing for the electors of St. John's Ward. He thanked, them one and all and it would be his endeavour to ' represent not only those who voted for him but the ' whole of the electors. (Cheers). Mr. POOE: said that when they began the electlna C 4 m paign they told the people they were going to fight the ward in the interests of Socialism. They gave the— , electors to understand that they were Socialists first , 1 Socialists second, and Socialists last. They had again las a Labour party had to meet all the organizations it was possible to bring in the field against a Labour candidate. They had had to fight Liberalism, Toryism, and the Non-Traders; and the Non-Traders, who had posed as the friends of Labour, had on every occasion I opposed the Labour candidate. The party which claimed to be the party of purity had during the present campaign adopted some of the worst methods it was possible to adopt in an election.. They had not been satisfied with doing what was upright and honest. They had told some or the biggest lies possible for anyone to tell, during the campaign. They had not only told lies deliberately, knowing that they were lies, but they had taken advantage of their ' power as property owners and had given men notice to quit—(A VOICE : That's a lie.) It was not a lie. They had given a man notice to quit a house because he , had taken a stand and worked for his class. That was the party of purity they knew to-day. There were many instances he could quote, but that was sufficient and he could prove every word he had said. They had used the ' worst weapons and done some of the worst actioos in the election, which a Labour man would never have ' stooped to. (Cheers and hooting). Mr. JAMES DODDS then addressed the crowd. He said he had fought a battle it Latchford as he believed, for the principle of the Non-Trading party, which was that no man should make a penny of profit from his position as a representative of the people in any kind of public capacity. He had fought the battle on principle. He did not fight for votes; he did not canvass ) a single elector himself, and he was satisfied with the result. If he had only received ten votes, he would . have said there were only ten people in Latchford who wanted purity in municipal life. (Cheers). He had no ill-feeling to anyone, and he was not going to make any complaint. He had had to fight the Conservative Association and the Liberal Association, or part of it, with the brewers and many teetotalers—a combination such as had perhaps never been known in the town, except when Couocillor Pemberton was opposed. He did not find any fault ; be would go on in the way he had gone on before, standing for a great principle, whether they returned him or not. " All I have to say now,"concluded Mr. Dodds, "is God save Warrington and God bless you all.' Good night." (Cheers.) Councillor OWEN TooLE said that as an independent he would endeavour to do his utmost on behalf of the ward and town. He must thank Mr. Morton for the gentlemanly manner in which he fought the election. They were friends before and they would be friends again. Councillor SANKEY said heivas sorry the people of Howley Ward had been put to any trouble and the town to an unnecessary expense by a contest. Councillor ASHCROFT also returned thanks and observed that he had never fought a more gentlemanly opponent than Mr. Halliwell.

At the Conservative Club. Mr, W. P. Rylands and the Non-Traders. Scenes of rejoicing were witnessed at the Conserva- tive Club, a large crowd assembling in the first-class billiard-room, and as Councillors Steel and Wilson entered they were greeted with enthusiastic cheers. Mr. W. P. RVLANDS voiced the satisfaction of the Conservative party at the victories which they had achieved. One feature of the elections was the great interest taken in the Non-Trading question. He was quite sure that probably all of them at one time were somewhat in favour of that movement but when its object had been very largely accomplished and all that remained was to safeguard it in the future the reason for the existence of the Non- Traders as a party disappeared. Instead of the Non- Traders retiring however,they found them entering into the field and attacking Conservative candidates with a bitterness which had never been equalled in any Radical attack made in the past. Posters had been put out and speeches uttered which were couched in the most bitter and aggressive language, and just because Councillor Wilson had taken a certain office to which a small emolument was attached and because he bad done what Mayor's auditors had previcusly done and accepted the remuneration he had been vituperated by the Non-Trading Party in a manner which was absolutely scandalous. Not content with that the Non- Traders brought out their great Goliath. The Philistines came forth to attack Councillor Wilson who, like the David of old, laid the giant low. Councillor WILSON returned thanks. At first, be said, he felt inclined to retire from the Council, but when he found that his personal character was being attacked he determined to fight to the bitter end. He would like to thank all who had worked for him and Particularly his old friend Councillor Luke Ellison. (Applause.) Mr. T. S. STEEL also returned thanks for his election,

WARRINGTON v. SALFORD.

Referee Awards a Penalty Try.

The postprmed League match between Salford and Warrington was played at Weaste on Wednesday before about 1,000 spectators. The weather was damp and gloomy, and the ball very bad to handle. Warrington were short of Isherwood at centre. Teams and officials:

..-- Salford—McWhirter„back; \Vallwork, Garner, Lomas and Hanpsom, three-quarter backs ; Brady and Preston, half-backs ; Dia Rees, E. Thomas, Williams, Brown, Lewis and Spencer, forwards. I Warrington—Berry, back; Fish, Cartwright. Taylor and Brookes,three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and Lees, half backs ; Preston, Shugars, Harmer, Heath Thomas, and Boardman forwards.

-...— Referee, Mr. W. IklcCutcheon, Oldham. Preston stated the game and the Warrington forwards rushed the ball to the line only to be penalised for off side. Salford at once went to the other end and Fish had ditliculty in clearing from a cross kick by D. Preston. Berry was penalised for not playing the ball, and Lomas kicked a fine goal. A similar chance came to Warriington five minutes later, but Fish failed. Salford were very aggressive and Preston breaking away sent the ball to Lomas who rushed over at the corner. Half time, Salford, 5 points ; Warrington nil. On resuming Salford were nearly over again in the opening rush. Warrington set to work with more energy and the hacks passed the ball with mere ability than before. Hockenhall made a fine effort, but his pass went astray. Warrington, however, kept up the attack, and a desperate run by Fish took play to the line wnere Harmer scrambled over, Preston epualising the scores with a good goal. Play now became very keen. Lomas broke through at centre and kicked the ball over the line. There was a race for possession between him and Taylor which the Warrington man won and dropped on the ball. To the consternation of War- rington the referee gave Lomas a try for obstruction. Brown failed at goal. In the concluding stages War- rington made great efforts to get level again, and Thomas went over under the posts but the try was disallowed. Final : Salford, 1 goal 2 tries (8 points) ; Warrington 1 goal I try (5 points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINOTON "A" v. SALFORD "A."

Prior to the cup-tie with Barrow on Saturday, Warrington "A" avenged the defeat sustained at the hands of Salford "A" a month ago. Neither side was at full strength but the home team played with greater dash. Shugars (2), Jones, Bradshaw. Swift, and Lambert scored tries, and Knight kicked three goals, before half-time. Ealford improved on resumption and had a try awarded for obstruction. Shortly after another try was scored from a forward rush.Warrington took the upper hand again, Hewitt, Jolley, and Forster crossing the line, Forster also kicking a goal. Final: Warrington 4 goals, 9 tries (35 points), Salford 2 tries (6 points.)

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALLEGED EMBEZZLEMENT.

On Saturday, at the Borough Police Court, a well- dressed young man named Harry Parrott, commission agent of 11, Fir-avenue, Runcorn-road, Moseley, Birmingham, was charged on a warrant with having embezzled a sum of £5 ss. on July 2nd, and a sum of £5 I 24. gd. on July 7th, moneys received by him on behalf of his employers, the British Soap Powder Company, Limited, Warrington. Mr. P. G. Taylor (Messrs. Greenall and Company) appeared to prosecute, and Mr. Browne defended.—Charles John Borrow, manager for the British Soap Powder Company, Liverpool-road, stated that prisoner, who was in the company's employ, forwarded them, on May 21st, an order for certain soap powders to be supplied to Mr. J. Roberts, of Birming- ham. Those goods were forwarded, but no money had been received for them by the prisoner,who was author- ised to collect moneys on behalf the con pany. Witness now produced a receipt signed by the prisoner, showing that Mr. Roberts paid prisoner £5 ss. for the goods on July 2nd. The receipt was no one of the company's cards.—Mr. Browne did not oppose the remand, but deprecated the fact that prisoner had been arrested on a warrant, contending that a summons would have been sulticient.—Prisoner was remanded until Tuesday next, bail of £5O being allowed.

BURGLARY AT WARRIRGTON.

" You seem to have adopted crime as a career," said Sir W. B. Forwood to James Towey, 42, labourer, who pleaded guilty to burglary at Warrington on October (ith,and to the theft of clothes and money belonging to Richard Milner, It was stated that prisoner hada year and seven days still to serve on a previous sentence of penal servitude.— (le Chairman pointed out that prisoner's 23 convictions included sentences of three and five years' penal servitude. The police had found him work on two occasions, and the only way to deal with such men cvas to keep them in confinement, where they could not do mischief.—Prisoner received twelve months' imprisonment with hard labour.

TICKET-OF-LEAVE MANS PLEA.

John Efurnberstone (39), a weaver, was indicted at the Liverpool AssipPs on Tuesday, for having at War- rington, on August 20th, broken into the lock-up shop of William Sherrin and stolen five razors, value 17s. 6d. —Prisoner pleaded that he had led a dog's life since be came out of penal servitude on a ticket-of-leave. His ticket had been a millstone round his neck, as he had no chance of getting work with it. He entered the shop through sheer destitution.—The Chai rrnan, in sentencing prisoner for nine months, remarked that he had had many opportunities to reform during his fifteen years' career of crime.

A NTEAN THEFT.

ig life

When the digestive organs are weak from sickness or illhealth they need abundant rest, as the invalid's weakened frame needs rest.

In growing vigour and failing power.

When strength is returning a care- fully regulated and increasing amount of exercise is beneficial.

What could be more to the purpose than a food of great nutritive value— a food which can be made suitable for any degree of digestive power, by the simple process of letting it stand for a longer or shorter period at one stage of its preparation?

For development and decline.

/n breaking health and convalescence.

Such a food, and the only food possessing this feature, is BENG ER'S FOOD, which is in this special way self-digesting.

What applies to the invalid can be urged with equal force in regard to the tender infant or the weakened functions of age. Benger's Fcod is dainty and delicious and most easily digested. Sold in tins by Chemists, etc., everywhere.

At the Warrington Poilce Court, on Thursday, James Higgins, Ship Yard, was charged with stealing two bundles of clothing, value 10s., from the White Hart Hotel, the property of William Turnbull. It appears on Saturday night a man of the farm-labouring class left his bundles behind, apparently having forgotten them. Prisoner visited the hotel on Monday night, saw the bundles, secreted behind a door, and during the absence of the landlord took them away. Prisoner was after- wards seen by Police-constable Rylatt carrying the bundles, and on visiting the house of his sister the cloth- ing was found concealed in various parts of the house. —The magistrates sent him to gaol for three months.

Warrington Overwhelm Barrow.

Barrow were the visitors to Warrington on Saturday in the second round of the Lancashire Senior Cup Competition and a crowd of quite 5,000 people assembled. The weather was fine and the conditions all in favour of a fast game. Warrington were handi- capped by the absence of both centres. Isherwood and Taylor, the substitutes being Cartwright and Lees. Barrow were short of Bamber on the wing, but other- wise at full strength. Teams and officials : Warrington.—Berry, back ; Fish, Cartwright. Lees, and Brooks, three-quarter hacks ; Hockenhall and Jordan, half backs ; Thomas, Preston, Heath, Harmer, Boardman and Naylor, forwards. Barrow.--Gifford, back ; Fox, Bowker, J. Brock- bank, and S. Brockbank, three-quarter backs ; Whar- ton and Watts, half backs ; Brough, Jardine, Harmer, Bowers, Hughes, and Rawlings, forwards. Referee. Mr. F. Kennedy, Broughton. Touch judges. Messrs. T. Smith, Leigh, and W. Platt, Swinton. Preston set the ball in motion, and play opened sensa- tionally. From the first scrummage the ball was passed across beautifully by Hoekenhall,to Cartwright and Lees and from Lees to Brooks. who dashed over at the corner amid deafening cheers. Fish added a goal,and the home lot were thus at once on good terms with themselves. Barrow were kept on the defensive, and five minutes later, Hockenhall after a tricky run to the left kicked across to Brooks, who scored again, Fish failing at goal. The visitors improved after this, and Bowker kicked a penalty goal. Warrington were always the superior team, and when Gifford had to retire with damaged ribs the chances of the Barrow men dwindled to nothing. Fish led the way to the line again with a splendid dribble which was completed by Heath scoring a try, Fish kicking a goal. Half-time : Warrington, 2 goals, 3 tries (13 points) ; Barrow, one goal (2 points). On resumption Barrow were still without Gifford but they set up a vigorous attack and Bowker dropped a goal. Fox next intercepted a pass in. his own half and after a long run scored a good try, which Bower con- verted. Warrington immediately dashed off to the other end and Hockenhall dodged over the line. Barrow responded with tine passing and Wharton scored under the posts, Bowker kicking another goal which brought up the visitors score to within 2 points of Warrington's. The horuesters then set to with determination and com- pletely overwhelmed the Barrow defence. In the course ,rif the next eight minutes 20 points were added, Thomas Lees, Jordan and Heath racing over, and Preston kick- ing four goals. There was a brief cessation of scoring and then Warrington finished up with another rush. Fish scored brilliantly and Preston obliged with another goal. Jordan added a try and a minute later, and just on time Brooks rushed over again. Final : Warrington 7 goals, 41 tries (47 points) ; Barrow 4. goals, 2 tries (14 points).l

RUNCORN WIN AT SkLFORD. Considerable interest was aroused in the meeting of these teams in the second round of the above com- petition, at the Willows, Weaste, and when Lomas started for the home side fully 8,000 9pectators were present. Little time was lost by the home side before

they were on the attack, and from neatly the half-way Lomas placed a magnificent goal. This reverse naturally caused the visitors to redouble their efforts, and Watton, snatching a pass from Jolley, safely grounded the ball over the line. but Richardson's kick was a \failure. On resuming, each side attacked in turn, and both lines underwent narrow escapes. Salford made stren- uous efforts to obtain the lead, but the visitors' defence was practically impregnable. The result of an extremey fast game was :—Runcorn 1 try (3 points). Salford 1 goal (2 points). Teams : Salford.—McWhirter, back ; Thomas, Garner, Lomas. and Hampson, three-quarter backs ; Preston and John, hacks; Rees, Thomas, Lewis, Foster, Williams, and Brown, forwards. Runcorn.—Harrison, back ; Flanagan, Moran, Butter- worth, and Deakin, three-quarter backs ; Jolley and Watton' half backs ; Walker, Richardson, Podbury, Dixon, Williams, aud Kennedy. forwards. Referee, Mr. M. E. Smirk, Wigan.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Trades and Labour Council and Pauper Labour.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. J. Billinge presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Murray, Dr. Patterson, Messrs. W. Hickman, S. Davies, R. Bolton, W. C. Brooks, M. W. Seton. W. Flannagan, A. Brooks, W. Houghton, E. L. Bent, R. Forshaw, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (asistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (workhouse master), and A. Wright (superintendent of the Padgate schools).

===== 0002601_19061103_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. RICKMAN'S QUALIFICATION.

Mr. HICKMAN said be wished so ask a question as to his own legal status as a Guardian. He was in doubt as to whether he was entitled to vote. He had been informed by an es-Guardian that he had no right to be present or to vote and a Resent Guardian told him that his vote would be challengeli The reason was that he had removed from the borough and his name would not appear on the new burgess list which came into opera- tion on Thursday. On the other band he had been told that his position held good qntil the end of the present Guardians' year which expiad in April and he would like the Clerk's definite ruling on the point. The CLERK thought it would be better if the Board could hear the objection of those who had said they would challenge the vote. No members, however, responded to this invitation and Mr. HICKMAN asked for the Clerk's opinion. The CLERK held that as Mr. Hickman had qualified for election by residence in the Union for more than one year preceding the election, he was not now dis- qualified from acting as a Guardian. At the end of the present Guardians' year, however, the qualification would cease and he would consequently not be able to seek re-election. TRADES COUNCIL AND PAUPER LABOUR.

The SECRETARY of the Trades and Labour Council (Councillor Stott) wrote stating that numerous com- plaints had come Ito his ears regarding certain things which were alleged to be practised by at least one member of the Board of Guardians. The complaint was that pauper labour was used from time to time to do the work of skilled artizans outside the Workhouse at a considerably less price Per hour than the recognized rate which obtained in Warrington. They would like the House Committee to make inquiries as to the truth or otherwise of these allegations. Mr. HICKMAN moved that the Board should resolve itself into Committee to deal with the question, but it was pitted out that the House Committee had carefully corisidered the subject, and the minutes were confirmed without further comment. THE ROMAN CATHOLIC CHAPLAIN.

The Board unanimously confirmed the resolution to pay the Rev. Father Ryan, in lieu of the annual pay- ments to him of £2O, and £3 for pew rents, the annual salary of 450 as Roman Catholic instructor in considera- tion of his conducting religious services and otherwise instructing Roman Catholic inmates of the Workhouse. The CLerac said he would submit the matter to the Local Government Board, although he hardly thought it required their approval. NEW OFFICES WANTED. The special committee appointed to consider the pro. vision of office accommodation for Relief District No. 3 recommended that the Guardians lease from Mr. J. G. Griffiths the premises No. 42, Church-street, for a term of 10 years at £3O a year. The CTERK advised thal if the Board agreed to the Committee's recommendation they should pay a yearly rent to cover all charges. He doubted whether they ha 4 authority to bind the ratepayers to a tenancy for ten years. He would, however, point out that for twenty years the Board had been moving in one direction, and that was to have the administrative and executive departments placed together in one building. He thought the Board were now in a fair position, without materially raising their debt, to do what other Unions icorresponding to their own had done, and have the Board and committee rooms, administrative and executive rooms and offices combined. They could not go on any longer in the present offices, and he suggested that the whole question be referred to the Sites Committee. Mr. lartoolis said the desire of the committee was to do away with the present inconvenient state of affairs n regard to the relief district in question, the relieving fficer living in one place and having his office in nother and his pay station in another. After a long discussion it was decided to refer the matter to the Special Committee (Miss Broadbent, Messrs. Mather, Jolley, and Brooks), the Sites Com- mittee (Messrs. Hickman, Jolley, Brooks, Houghton, and Dr. Patterson) together with Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, the Chairman, Mr. Bent, and Mr. Forshaw.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. HAVELOCK WILSON'S AFFAIRS. Meeting —olireditors.

On Thursday, at London Bankruptcy Court, the creditors met under the failure of Mr. J. Havelock Wilson, M.P. for Middlesborough, described as of Maritime Hall, West India Dock-road, London, and of Fairmount-road, Brixton, London. The Chairman said that a statement of affairs-had been lodged showing insecured liabilities £1,031 and no assets. The receiving order was made on Augustf23rd. The debtor stated that he had held the office 91 president of the Sailors' and Firemen's Union for about stateen years, since its foundation, his remuneration being £3 10s. a week. Apart from that he had no 11 employment or source of income. He attributed his prqent position to his long-standing liabilities on behalf of the union, which had been revived against him upon his re-election to Parliament. The Official Receiver added that a receiving order had previonsly been made against Mr. Havelock Wilson about 1893, but it was shortly afterwards rescinded. He asked if any proposal was now made. It was stated on the debtor's behalf that he was desirous of submitting a proposal for the payment of a cash composition of 102. in the pound in satisfaction of their claims. _ _ .

The meeting was adjourned until the 15th inst., in order that the pioposal might be duly lodged and circulated.

MOUSTACHES IN THE ARMY.

In consequence of it having been observed that several officers in the Irish command are in the habit of shav- ing the upper lip, General Lord Grenfell has directed attention to paragraph 660 of the King's Regulations, which orders for all ranks "that chin and under lip are to be shaved, but not the upper lip. Whiskers, if worn, are to be of moderate length."

MR. CHANIBERLAIN AND WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE.

A lady at Streatham, London, having written to Mr. ! Austen Chamberlain askin4 him if there was any truth in the published statement that he and his father were in favour of women's suffrage, on Tuesday received the following reply : " I am not in favour of women's , suffrage, nor, I believe, is Mr. Chamllerlain." IThe will of Miss nary Ann Swarm, of Hirst-road, Bedlington, an elderly lady who lived by herself and was brutally murdered last August, has been proved for 2005.

• MONEY-LENDER GETS A PENNY A WEEK. I An order for payment at the rate of one penny a week was made at Wigan on Tuesday in a case in which a widow was sued by a money-lender named Mark Tarshish, carrying on business in the town, for £1 15s. balanze owing on a pronlissory note signed by her late husband. Judge Bradbury. after a little calculation on I paper, remarked that the interest charged had been at ; the rate of 90 per cent. By another little calculation it will be found that the debt will take 21 years and 48 weeks to repay.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, My post-bag this week is, I think, a lit, beyond the usual size, but at the same timL there is not the variety in it which I have re- ceived in the past. This week it has been all stories, and some of them rather long. This means that many of my little friends' contri- butions get pigeon-holed several weeks before they appear in print. I would like in future to receive stories of about 200 words in length, short poems, anecdotes, etc.

I expect a great many of you know to a day how close Christmas is getting, and I would like to suggest that you all put on thinking- caps and send me some original contributions, taking the form of a seasonable conundrum, a new Christmas game—in fact, anything suitable for a jolly Christmas party—for instance, the following is rather new to me: You ask a friend if he can remember fourteen easy words; naturally he thinks he can, anti answers yes. So you ask him to repeat the following:—

"Good morning, madam!" to Eve said Adam; "Good morning, Adam!" to him said madam. and the attempts your friend make in trying to repeat it correctly will positively make a cat laugh! Try it. Well, to continue, I want to get a goodecollection of Christmassy jokes, etc., ready for the C.C.L. Christmas letter, so I want you all to send me something by the end of November—never mind what—but some- thing—a joke, game, trick—anything which will cause fun; and for the three best things I will give three nice Yuletide gifts. I shall not tell you thidi week what I intend giving, but the best pride will .go for the best cwitribution, that is to say; if the trick is bette/ than the riddle, then the trick gets the B—, there, I nearly let it out, but it's not a bicycle. I am very pleased with the result of the girls' needlework competition, and have great pleasure in awarding prizes as follows :—lst, Dora Vigor; 2nd, Elsie Lewis; 3rd, Olive Kingdon. I am sorry I cannot give prizes to all who com- peted; however, perhaps their turn will come some day. UNCLE NED.

I have this week to announce the member- ship of four nieces and one nephew. (I expect the future nephews are still, after those "conqueror fives," etc.) So I Lave pleasure in introducing Edith Eley, Alice Sadd, May Budden, Lily Oldham, and Fred Squires into our happy "Corner."

Dear Uncle Ned,—Every week I look out for your "Corner," so, as I am ten years old now, I thought I should like to join. I thought I should like to write a story to-day. I hope you will like it, for it is called "Harry's Surprise." Hoping you are quite well, with love from

MAY BUDDEN.

HARRY'S SURPRISE.

Harry Paul was a litle boy of seven years, and he had two little sisters and another brother not quite so old as himself. Now, on the 14th of August was his birthday, and he would be eight years old then, so when/his birthday came his father had decided to give him two little pet dogs, and his mother Some white rabbits; and his brother and sisters wanted to give him something, because when they had a birthday he always gave them something, and their mother said: "You can give him a hen and some chickens; he would like some of those, and besides that he would have some eggs, and he could sell them." "Yes," said the children, "he would like that." So Dadda fumbled in their pockets and brought out some money, and said to the children. "Supposing we get them for you?" " Yes, please! Dadda," they Ensweyed. The next day was Harry's birthday, and .when father came home he said: "Dadda, I haven't got any presents at all!!! "Oh " said Dadda; "When breakfast is finished, come out in the garden and feed your dogs and rabbits, and hen and chickens; if there are any eggs you can sell them to mamma." " Oh, no!" said Harry; "she is welcome to those when she wants them." So out they all went to feed them.

The next contributor to the " Corner " is Fred Squires, who sends the following:— Dear Uncle Ned,—l take a great interest in reading the "Bideford Gazette," which is a weekly paper, and I have found the "Children's Corner" to be the most interesting of all the news. lam fourteen years of age, and I should like to be a member of your League. I am sending you a short story of my own, called : "Pride comes before a fall." Hoping I shall be successful, I am sending you a drawing, too. With love from your loving nephew, FRED SQUIRES.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIDE COMES BEFORE A FALL.

'Two little boys were seen one day out in a field; one was the son of rich parents, and had the best of everything; the other was a poor boy who had no father, and whose mother was a drunkard. The rich boy's name was Thomas, an the poor boy's name was John.

aile they were out la a field, Thomas began brag and show off his clothes to the poor soy, who, seeing that he had on ragged clothes, Jegan to cry, While he was crying the other boy was despising him. When John stopped crying, he said in gentle words: "My poor daddy, who is dead and buried, used to work hard for his living, and did not boast like you; hie used to say to me, "Pride comes before a fall." Thomas said, "You are talking nonsense." John said, "No I am not." While they walked on, they came to some barb wire, and John was the first to go over, and when he was over the other side of the wire he turned round and said, "Don't you rip your clothes, Tom," and was going to help him over, but Tom said, "Go away, I don't want help from tramps," so John let go his hand. Thomas, who was boasting that he could go over, got one of his legs over, and was going to put the other over, when he over- balanced himself, and, falling on a stone, was killed instantly.

John went h-ome to Thomas' house and told his father that his son was lying dead over in a field. His father went over, and, after a few minutes' walk, he came to the dead body of his son.

He took the body up, and carried it home, giving John. a ss. piece for helping him. So let this be a motto for all boys and girls: "Pride comes before a fall."

PEED SQUIRES.

Dear Uncle Ned,—Wishing to be a new mem- ber of the "Children's Corner," I am forward- ing you this story.

Your affectionate niece,

LILY OLDHAM.

THE TWO WAIFS.

Once upon a time a rich gentleman had a little son, whom he loved very much. One day the little boy was playing in the garden, when some gipsies came by.

When the gipsies saw the litle boy, one of them climbed over the gate and took him away with them.

As soon as they got a long way from his home they took off his clothes and put on some old ones.

While he was with the gipsies he played with a little girl a few years older than himself, who had also been stolen from her home.

One day, when the gipsies had be beating . them, they both made up their minds to run , away and try to find their mother and father_ The next day they both went out to play, and off they ran along the lane in which they had :been brought up by the gipsies. On the way , the little boy fell and hurt his leg. Just then a man came by with a horse and cart' who L knew where the little boy lived, so he put them in his cart, and just before he reached his house he lifted the children out and showed them the way. Soon they came to a large house, and the little boy recttnised it as the house where his father lived. Soon his father came out, and when he saw his, little boy he picked him up and ran to his Wife, who was in the garden, never taking any notice of the litle girl. The little girl stood outside and cried bitterly when she saw her little playmaft had. gone. Soon the gipsies, who had been looking for them, came by, and when they saw her the, picked her up and beat her.

While the gipsies had been taking the little. girl, her playmate had been telling the whole story. Soon the little boy and his father came out to see if the little girl had gone, but they ' were just too late, as the gipsies had carrier/ her away. Soon they set out to find her, but it took them a long time before they succeeded. Just as they reached the house the man want', to hit the little girl, but his wife would not him, and so he threw the lamp at her; another Moment the house was in flames. The little boy's father rushed upstairs, and there in one corner of the room lay the little girl, un- conscious. He carried her outside to the little boy, and when he saw her he danced for joy. They took her to his home, and fifteen years after they got married, and always lived happily together.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 3, 1906.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

GENERATING ELECTRICITY AT THE PIT'S MOUTH.

Of recent years a good deal has been., said about generating electricity at the pit's lnouth, and transmitting it to various industrial centres. But it would be considerably cheaper, declares the Scientific American, to manufacture producer gas at the pit's mouth and transmit it through pipes to the industrial centres, there to use it for driving gas engines for generating electricity and also for heating purposes and furnace work. The questions of the distribution and transmis- sion of power miist not be confused. For the former it is agreed that there is no agent *to compare pvith electricity: For the latter purpose it is suggested that it is more economical to em- ploy producer-gas and piping than electricity an cables.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
BEARING ALLOYS.

With regard to a note quoted Iron: the rut- , can respecting bearing alloys, in which the writer states that Messrs. Siemens and Halske have In- troduced a new bearing metal composed to a large extent of cadmium and antimony, which is described as being evolved owing to the present very high cost of tin, a well-known metal com- pany write to point out that the cost of the alloy referred to must be prohibitive for general purposes, owing to the expense of The metal cadmium, described as its principal component? It will be quite sufficient to state that the price of cadmium is approximately 4s. per pound, which would absolutely debar the use of any metal containing it in any large proportion for any but special uses.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
FOSSILS AND GOLD.

In Alaska the , bones, and 'often the entire bodies, of extinct animals, such ,as the mam- moth, the mastodon, the reindeer, and the bison, are ,found most abundantly in the layers of soil directly above the gold-bearing gravels. So inti- mate is this association between fossil animal re- mains and the auriferous deposits that M. Obal- ski, who lately explored Alaska, says that the fossils serve the prospectors as indices of the metallic richness of the soil. In ancient times both the gold and the bones and bodies of the animals were deposited at the bottoms of valleys by the action of rivers and smaller streams, many of which have now disappeared. Consequently the appearance of fossils is an almost certain in- dication that gold will be found in the neigh- bourhood. The cabins of many miners are or- namented with huge tusks and antlers unearthed in the diggings.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
UTILISATION OF LIQUID AIR.

' Some curious facts in connection with liquid air have been placed on record by Dr. Linde, shewing, that when liquid air is allowed to evapo- rate freely the nitrogen evaporates more rapidly than the oxygen. For instance, it was found that the vapour which first appeared from the decomposition of liquid air contained only 7 per cent, of oxygen and 93 per cent, of nitrogen. When three-quarters of the liquid had evapo- rated it consisted of half oxygen and half nitro- gen; but, when evaporated down to one twenty- fifth, the residue contained 90 per cent, of oxygen, or about one-sixth of the original quan- tity. It is stated that a process has been in- vented by Dr. Linde by' which perfectly pure oxygen and nitrogen respectively can he ob- tained. The uses to which nitvegen can be put are now fairly familiar to the public. Whether it can be economically produced by the method referred to as a fertilising agent remains to be seen. Tho utilisation of oxygen, however, is now extending far beyond the therapeutic pur- poses to which it was formerly confined. As an agent in the production of high temperatures it is useful for a wide variety of purposes, though the cost of its distributiOn—whicn is stated to be considerably greater than that of production —is a deterrent to its more general employment.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
MOVEMENT OF FISHES' EYES.

M. Nishikawa, in the Japan Zoological Anno- tations, describes a mode by which the eye. of the lower side of a flat fish travels round to the upper side during metamorphosis. According to the observations of Agassiz, the eye of the eventually blind side of the majority of flat fishes travels round the dorsal edge of the head until it attains its final position, and it is not until the rotation is completed that the dorsal fin grows forward beyond the level of the eyes An exception in this respect is Plasrusia, in which the dorsal, fin grows forward to the snout, while the eyes are still symmetrical, and the left eye attains its final position on the left side of the fin by penetrating through the soft tissues at the base -qf the fin. In the fish observed by M. Nishikawa the dorsal fin also grows forward -before the rotation of the right eye; but this anterior extension does not unite with the head until after the rotation is completed. Its neutral margin 'is contiguous with the dorsal surface of the head posteriorly where there is a distinct hole, bounded by the head and the anterior ex- tension of the fin, for the passage of the right eye, which travels round the dorsal side of the , head without sinking into its tissues. It is thus clear that the mode by which the change is effected in this fish is intermediate between that observed in ordinary flat fishes and that which is exhibited by Plagusia.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
ADAPTABIIITY OF SPIDERS.

M. A. Locaillon has p'ublished in the Revue Srientifique a series of articles on the instincts and mental development of the spider. On the whole he is not satisfied that they attain a very high degree of perfection, except in what are called their instincts—i.e., the phenomena of adaptation which they present. • We cannot say that the -spider is exactly what one would call an engaging creature. It is easily excited to pas- sion, and this often proves its undoing. To catch the tarantula in Nicaragua a string with a ball of soft wax at the end is lowered into its hole. The spider resents this intrusion on its privacy, and buries its formidable jaws in the wax, to its own destruction. The trap-door spider of New Zealand can always be teased by a straw into do- ing battle. This creature has been accused of suicide, for it is often. found to have deliberately sealed up its nest and to have Ili& perished with its young. Fear seems to produce in the spider a kind of bewllderment.• Thus in fleeing from the foraging ant the Nicaraguan spiders will run, not away from, but straight towards their enemies. They seem to have an odd diversity of attitude to certain kinds of food, Thus it has been noticed thai one spider will refuse to eat a certain butterfly which another will greedily do. ,our. In some countries it is so sensitive to at- mospheric influences that it takes the place of a barometer. Intelligence, industry, ingenuity, perseverance, and cunning they shew in 'a marked degree. Some can make bolts for their doors, and others construct a self-acting hinge. They know when their web has been weakened, vat.; its structure with its position, test its security, and make the necessary repairs. The power of adaptation to special circumstances as they arise is very notable in the Archnida.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MICROSCOPY OF METALS.

Professor. J. A. Ewing has published some most interesting speculations on the subject of the yielding of metals under Mechanical strain. For many years this field was barren of real scientific conclusions. It. was known, of. course, that metals would yield if strained be. yond a certain point. This point could roughly be specified in the case of a particular sample of metal prepared in a specified manner. But that was almost all Isays the Hon. R. A. Strutt). The subject. seemed to make no progress. En- gineers were chiefly concerned with the results of such rough tests as have been mentioned, which sufficed for many of the purposes of en- gineering design. Many ptrzzling facts were, boWever, known Which required some new me- thod of research properly to co-ordinate and ex- plain them. Such a method is found in the appli- cation of the microscope to metallurgy. Prof esor Ewing has studied in particular the c-dieet which is produced on this crystalline struc- ture .by straining the metal. He has found that when the metal is strained beyond that point at which elastic recovery is possible the crystal yields by a process of .slipping, parallel to some definite plane, as, for instance, a pack of cards will slip when a tangential force is applied to the top of the pack. This illustration is, however, only partial. For there is cohesion be- tween the layers of the crystal, while there is none between the successive cards of the pack. The remarkable point/ is that' this cohesion is not destroyed by the oechrrence of slip such as bas been described. When , one plane cif the crystal slides over another, a molecule which was fo:-- merlY joined to its neighbour in the tactical ar- rangement loses this position. It - would seem to take up its position opposite a new neighbour after the slip has occurred, and to be attached to it in the same manner. It appears, however, that this does not take place in the case of every individual molecule: and the more often the pro- cess is reneated the more molecules get into dis- array. This produces a weakening .of the ma- terial. .Here we. have an explanation of the long familiar fact Of the weakening of metal parts which are alternately strained in elongation and us comnression—such, for instance, as the niston rode of, steam engines. slip occurs to and tro iu the crystals, and each time more and more mole- cules fail to recover that proper alignment in the crystal which gives the material its strength. Thus progressive weakening occurs.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Latchford Ward. Sir,—The recent election in the Latchford Ward has been an interesting one from many points of view. The spectacle of Liberals, Conservatives, Good Templars, and brewers, all united in their universal dread of Mr. Dodds being returned is surely a remarkable one. Verily, the lion and the lamb lying together. I think poor Mr. Wilson was quite overwhelmed by the force behind him, and personally I feel rather sorry for him. It is a bit hard on a man, when his demerits are con- sidered of more value than his merits, and when the story that he was in need of monetary assistance is the best argument for his accepting it. One objection we heard raised against our candidate was, that he created rows. Was there over a reformer who didn't? Cromwell was hardly a mild type of man, and Cobden and Bright might be considered somewhat disturbing elements. However, the blindness of the present elector seems to prevent him realising that, in opposing the Non-Trading move- ment, he is his own worst enemy. Let us hope that a future occasion will enable him to see clearly through the fog of distorted facts, and circulars, more remark- able for the " autocratic balderdash " they contain than for any items of truth they may present ; one of which would certainly have carried more weight had it been signed by its ingenious author, instead of, what, at first sight, seemed to be the whole of the Liberal Execu- tive, but was, after all, merely " a ?member." In conclusion, let me say that we in Latchford do not lose heart, but remember that—" we fall, to rise are baffled—to fight better, sleep—to wake !"—Yours etc., A LATCHFORD NON-TRADEtt.

St. John's Ward Election. Sir,—There were a good•many stories circulated dur- ing the campaign against Mr.Maginn—which stories the smooth-tongued politician now describes as" termino- logical inexactitudes." Others, more plainly spoken, describe them by the use of a much shorter word. One of these stories at least, however, should not be allowed to travel any further, for it has caused and will cause considerable heart-burnings—to say nothing of other things. The story was freely circulated that Mr. Maginn had attacked and made disparaging remarks about his own particular Faith and Church. This was a gross slander. The only references Mr. Maginn made to his Church, were those when he men- tioned the incident of his opponent's visit—and also when pleading the cense of Free Education be stated that the only free schools in Warrington were the Roman Catholic schools. As a matter of fact Mr. Maginn abso- lutely forbade those who spoke from his platform to introduce party politics and religion. He stood out for and fought straightforwardly as an independent candi- date.He actually declined the assistance for this reason of some men who could have rendered valuable service. I have watched his career for some years—it is such an interesting 01:10-but have only been brought into close contact with him during the past few days. I am not at all surprised to find that those who know him best and have his friendship almost worship the man, for his personal magnetism is extraordinary. He is singularly free from " cant " and " humbug." No ordinary man could have risen as he has done. When one considers that he came into War- rington some years ago, with not even in his pocket so much as the proverbial Gd., worked for I Is. a week, slept in a hayloft, and from that lowly position rose by sheer physical exertion and mental power to become an employer of several hundreds of men and one of the leading men in the town; that in itself is sufficient evidence of the calibre of the man who has made Warrington the town of his adoption. If there'were only more men of the stamp of Mr.JohnMaginn Warrington would be a happier and more prosperous town than it is. My only object, however, at the pre- sent moment is to help to clear away a slanderous story and to say—if any one, at least ought to be proud of Mr. John Maginn, it is those of his own particular Faith and Church. For my own part, it has been a distinct pleasure to have come into so close a contact with such a broad-minded Catholic.—Yours truly, E. ENGLAND.

===== 0002601_19061103_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Dodds and the Elections.

Sir,—On the whole I am well satisfied with yester- day's results. The Non-Traders have not only held their own, but of the nine seats just refilled, three are now held by members of our Association, and four by supporters of our main objects. We were attackers in only one Ward, and that because the retiring member bad wrongly and as we are advised, accepted payment as Mayor's AUditor, and not because he was a Conservative, as is suggested by the Conservatives' Chairman. When the money was received we publicly announced that the issue would be raised yesterday, altogether irrespective of the result. Our candidate's health broke down at the last moment and I was induced to take his place. We fought for principle rather than for votes, and in view of the great pressure brought to bear by the Conservative Executive and by the Liberal Executive (or certain members thereof) backed by the traders, brewers, and certain teetotallers, I am proud that so many as 461 indepen- dent electors were found to be staunch enough to stand by us and the principle we represent, especially as I did not personally canvass a single voter. In the Conservative Executive's circular sent out with Mr. Wilson's poll card, it is alleged that the Non- Traders have waited till a working man has taken a fee as Mayor's auditor before taking exception to the pay- ment. This is entirely misleading, because since the Non-Traders came into the Council no one has had a fee as Mayor's Auditor before Mr. Wilson accepted .£3O a few weeks ago. The Chairman of the Conservative Executive may as well realise at once that the Non-Traders are no res- pectors of persons nor of parties, and that if he or any member of his party acts as Councillor Wilson has done he will have to defend his position. In reply to a placard, whose author by the way is a Conservative, the Conservative Chairman, while con- demning doubt in Non-Traders is of a suspicious turn himself, as is proved by his suggestion that "the Non- Trading party is a political organisation working directly against the Conservative Cause." I challenge him to find a tittle of evidence to prove that the Non-Traders Association has ever shown the slightest party inclina- tion. lam the only Liberal among its leaders and I I defy him to give one single instance where f have shown any disposition, in respect of the Non-Trading issue, to favour my own side. It is delightful to see that the Conservative Chair- man is opposed to what is known as illegal and immoral trading by members of the Council with the Corpora- tion ; if he will add to this the receiving of profit or payment in any way whatever, and will induce his party in Warrington to adopt these principles, and if the Liberal Chairman and party will do the same, the occupation of the Non-Traders, like Othello's, will be • gone. Both parties in Warrington will have to come • to this sooner or later, why not now?—Yours &c., JAMES DODDS. November 2nd, 1,06.

" Gas Worker's " Reply to Mr. Morton. Sir,—Mr. Morton still continues in the tiresome occupation of beating the air. Words and nothing but words. In my last reply I pointed out the action of Liberal Labour representatives in supporting the Liberal Government of 1893, in their refusal to consider the question of unemployment, although 3,000,000 wbre suffering from the distress cared by lack of work and in 1903, when a similar condition of affairs had arisen in the industrial world the Liberal Labour M.P.'s marched into the same lobby with their official Liberal allies and supported what they previously opposed. I stated as an explanation of this inconsistent conduct that in 1593 Liberals were in office, in 1903 they were in opposition. And still Mr. Morton claims Liberal Labour members "have acted independently." Independent and united political action is the only hope ,of the worker. It is the key to justice and right. The Liberal Labour alliance has been a miserable failure. After weary years of docility and confidence, out of an .election chamber of close on 700 members and a heredi- tary Chamber exclusively composed of capitalists and landlords " our friends from 1871 to the recent -General Election very indulgently permitted Labour on the average about 10 seats. And I may mention when Mr. Broadhurst, Mr. Havelock Wilson, and the late Mr. Macdonald and a few others, attempted to attain Par- liamentary honours, they had to fight the relentless opposition of Liberals and Conservatives for having the hardihood to interfere with the wealthy few ancient's preserves. Mr William Abraham. M.P.,president of the South Wales Miners Federation, writing in the "South Wales News" last month, on the vote given at the Trade Union Congress in favour of Labour unity on independent lines said :—" Whatever may be our objection to the ways and means of the present Labour Party formed under the auspices of the L.R.C. of arriving at the goal, there can be no real objection i and there s not, on part of Labour men already in Parliament to a scheme for the formation of a Labour Party independent of the two great political Parties which in their turn have held power over the British electors." This statement I consider a frank admission that Liberal Labour is under the arbitrary domination of Liberalism and Mahon" has sufhcient political perception to see that it is only a question of a few Tears of all organised industrial workers arraying them- selves unitedly for common objects under the banner of Independence. The pioneers, or those in the conflict at present may not see the fruits of their labours accomplished, the return to power of a Labour Govern- went, but we agree with the Liberal "Wakefield Echo" when it said on 2nd December 1904, "The Labour Party is evidently not to be denied. If they cannot get into power themselves they are determined that those who do hold the reins of office shall not slacken their grip of public questions and in their capacity they may be a very useful body, acting as a sort of whip to quicken national and local authorities and keep them alive to the real needs of the community."—Yours,etc., GAS WORKER. I:This correspondence must now cease.—ED. "Ex."]
