VOTES FOR WOMEN.

CAUSE SUPPORTED AT WARRINGTON

Rev. J. F. Matthews and Miss

Pankhurst.

The cause of woman sufferage has in the minds of the general public, become synonymous with disorder, but whilst the actions of a certain section of the suffragettes are open to question and criticism there is a strong undercurrent of feeling, especially in the ranks of the women workers of the country which:evidences itself by quiet but none the less determined work to achieve the enfranchisement of the fair sex. One of the leaders of what might be termed the orthodox agitators, Mrs. Allan Bright, visited Warrington on Wednesday, and at a meeting in the Cairo-street schools, she won many eonverts to the cause which she has at hearti There was an excellent attendance over which Mr. C. E. Parker presided. Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., had been asked and had promised to take the chair, but he wrote expressing his regret that, acting on the advice of his medical attendant, he was not able, at present to attend any public meetings, and that he was therefore unable to be present to support the cause of woman suffrage as advocated by the Liberal ladies of Warrington. Mr. PARKER said that whilst they might have found a more able chairman for the afternoon he was sure that they could not have found anyone more in through sympathy with the objects which they had in view. He did not think "poor man"constituted theempediment in the way of woman suffrage bet that their real difficulty lay in the indifference of the fair sex themselves. "I do not think" he continued "that anyone who gives serious thought to the question, can deny the right of the claim of the women to be put on an equality with men in regard to the franchise. Women have to bear the responsibily of citizenship ; they have to pay the taxes and are amenable to the law, and it is manifestly unjust that the privileges of citizenship should not accompany the responsibility, and I do not see that the indifference of a very large number of women is suffi- cient reason why the claim should not be granted." Continuing he said it was only by ventilating the sub- ject and convincing these who were opposed—and he did not think they were many—that their claim could be successfully put forward. (Hear, hear.) "The inequalities " he said " not only in regard to the franchise but in many other things, which , arise from difference in sex are relics of barbarism ;, relics of the time when the physical superiority of man was the dominating force. With the evolution of the race mind has more and more become the ruling force and as man's mental equal or even superior, woman should be placed on an equality with those of the apposite sex." (Applause.) Mr. Parker proceeded to illustrate how woman, through her inability to influence legislation suffered `grevious wrongs ' emphasising in particular sweating, and contending that woman was much more the victim of industrial slavery than men. "The way in which some women are expressing their opinions "continued the Chairman " will probably not commend themselves to many. As a law abiding man I depricate any reform being advocated by the breaking of the law but Whilst some of us object to the manner in which the case of women suffrage is being pushed to the front we have to recognize that these women are expressing an earnest conviction." Mr. Parker proceeded to urge the holding of a big demonstration in the Parr Hall,at which they should ask Miss Pankhurst to speak. The suggestion bad a mingled reception some support- ing and °tilers objecting to the name of Miss Pankhurst, but all supported the Chairman when he said that they wanted to get at the people who were indifferent to the cause. _ •

Mrs. ALLAN BRIGHT, who proudly described herself as one of the " old guard " in the campaign for woman suffrage spoke of the hopes and disappointments which the advocates of the cause had felt. They had, she said toiled along the weary road until they were within sight of their object when they received their first check, by the Act in 1899 which converted the London vestries into the Borough Councils. She explained how women served, and with marked success, on the vestries, but how by the law of the land they were debarred from sitting on the Borough Councils although those authorities did exactly the same work as the vestries. The exclusion of women was a distinct loss to the poor of London, and especially to the women and the children, and by no means a gain to the general ratepayers. The same thing had transpired in the matter of education. Women were entitled to sit on the old School Boards as elected representatives, whilst they could only now be co-opted on to the education authorities. She need not empha- size the great difference between the first and the last. Co-opted members only sat by favour of the Committee whilst elected representatives worked with the whole force of the electorate behind them. Those two checks affected different constituions differently. Some I retired from the struggle, others met them with a calm' resignation, whilst the more intemperate ones insisted, upon rushing to their destination. To speak in metaphor I the third party dashed along in unlicensed motor cars without lights, pursued by policemen and driving reck- lessly over every barrier and obstacle. She would take them to a scene in the Hcase of Commons when on May 12th, 1905, a Woman's Suffrage Bill was to be introduced, but another insignificant bill about the lighting of vehicles stood in the way. She and 40 other ladies sat in the ladies gallery, and saw a few scoffing men play with the Lighting of Vehicles Bill until it was too late for the other measure to be introduced. They after- wards left in sorrow and silence. She would ask them to picture another scene in the House of Commons on that memorable occasion when a few women thrust a flag through the grille and waved defiance to an out raged and astonished house (Mr. Matthews "Hear, hear.") Before those ladies were judged she would ask her hearers to remember that there were certain terh- peraments whose enthusiasm carried them away, and they must also remember that not a single great reform had been carried by absolutely orthodox and quiet methods. (Hear, hear.) The iron entered into the souls of those who went to prison and her only fear I was that those women would come out blind to every- I thing but one cause and that some day a great tragedy might occur which would get them the suffrage but at a very serious price. (Hear, hear.) How could they prevent that enthusiasm being carried too far? Certainly not by abusing women who were suffering for their conscience sake. Miss Pankhurst— that brilliant young lady who had been admitted as a barrister but who by the law of the land could not practice—assured her that she would never rest silent until the reform was carried. "I tell you frankly," said Mrs. Bright, " that I have no hopes of securing the franchise from either the Conservative or the Labour party, but I look confidently to the new party—the Labour party—to give us the measure. We ask for no fancy fyanclaise. We want the vote on exactly the same lias lines as the men." In conclusion Mrs. Bright appealed to all women for support in the work. (Applause). Mrs. PARKER, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs. Bright for her address, spoke of the general ignorance on the question and said she would like to remove one one false impression. They did not desire to have women in the House of Commons. _ .

Mr. R. LLOYD JONES seconded. He agreed with Mr. Parker's suggestion in regard to a Parr Hall meeting and thought they should endeavour to secure the services ,of the healthiest of the women at present in prison. (Laughter and applause). Alderman BE'NNETT pointed out that he had for many years been a supporter of the cause of women's suffrage, and that, about a dozen years ago, he issued a leaflet to Conservatives in which the main plank in the pro- gramme was votes for women. It was not a party ques- tion at all, but was a matter of essential justice. (Applause.) The Rev. J. F. MATTEws said he would dearly like to see women in Parliament. He would like to see Miss Pankhurst on the floor of the House of Commons dress- ing down the weak-kneed Liberals and the long-faced Tories. The vote was heartily accorded, and a similar com- pliment was paid to Mr. Parker for his services in the chair. Refreshments were served at the close. The secretary of the local Branch of the Women's Suffrage Leagne is Miss Waring, Latchford. In his letter to the Secretary, Mr. Crostleld said : From my previous letters you will know how sorry and disappointed I am that I cannot join you at the meeting to-morrow evening in order to hear Mrs. Bright's address, and to support Female Suffrage to the extent and on the lines on which it is advocated by the Liberal ladies of Warrington. It is a great disappointment to me. I had counted on being quite up to the mark by the opening of Parliament, but unfortunately the doctor here says that after a pretty heavy strain on me for the last few years, I need to give myself several weeks of complete rest, and I am hoping to be able to take some of this after the Trades Disputes Bill this session, and the remainder during the vacation. I do grudge missing any of this Session, as it is likely to be so very interest- ing, but Sir Victor Horsley says .1 need a long breathing space, after which to make a fresh start at politics, and unfortunately he is right. Incidentally he has forbidden me temporarily attempting public meetings. Please give my best compliments to Mrs. Bright, and tell her how very sorry I am to miss hearing her.

Church Clubs' Billiard League. In the first division of the above League those two old rivals, Trinity and St. Pauls, met on Thursday evening at the abode of the former club. St. Paula received a bad defeat, their opponents retiring victor- ious with the large majority of 229. Scores : Trinity : W, Leigh 75, W. Baines 75, J. Leigh 75, E. Hesketh 75, R. Walsh 75, J. Evans (captain) wO. 75, total 450. St. Paul's: T. Hill 60, W. Bennett 25, F. Gardner 44, T. Addison 46, A. C. Binks (captain) 46, P. Parkes nil. Total 221. Majority for Trinity Ist. 229. The matches between St. Clements and Pad gate Ist. and 2nd teams have been postponed until the 22nd inst.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 3, 1906.

MR. CARNEGIE'S GIFT.

PROVISIONALLY ACCEPTED BY THE TOWN COUNCIL.

Disappointment at the Amount.

Mr. Andrew Carnegie's gift of £3,500 for the erection of a branch library was arxepted provisionally at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tues- day evening. There was, however, considerable discus- sion on the matter._ _ .

The agenda contained the intimation that ate meeting of the Museum Committee a letter was read from Mr. Carnegie offering to give 0,500 to build a oranchlibrary on condition that the Council provide a site and impose an additional rate of Id. In the £ for maintenance. It was resolved, that the offer made be accepted provision- ally ; also that the sub-committee inquire whether the Corporation can provide a site. Alderman BENNETT, who moved the adoption of the minutes, said his views on the Carnegie gift were well known, but he was now speaking as the mouthpiece of the committee. _ _ .

The MAYOR said nobody knew anything about the matter, and be thought they ought to send it back sod let the new Council deal with it. It would have been better if the Museum Committee had let them have the correspondence and all information about the matter. (Hear, hear.) Alderman BEYsinT said he was quite prepared to lay the facts before the meeting. The MAYOR : You have never taken us into your confidence. . _ .

Alderman BEIs'NETT said they would remember that the original request made to Mr. Carnegie was for a gift which would be sufficient to enable them to build two branch libraries at a cost of from .£3,000 to £3,000 each, and they also requested from him a gift sufficient Ito extend the accommodation at the Museum, adding to the Library and providing more rooms in connection with the institution. These matters naturally moved very slowly, but the committee had very carefully considered I them from time to time. They received the following letter from Mr. Carnegie :—" Skibo Cas'.le, Dornock, Sutherland, July 13th, 190 G. Arthur Bennett, Esq., Chairman Free Library, Municipal Museum, Warrington. Dear Sir,—Yours of 28th June received. Mr. Carnegie is not disposed to consider the extension of the Central Library Building nor the erection of two branch library buildings, but he will he glad to pay thirty-five hundred pounds sterling for the erection of one branch library building provided Council impose an additional one farthing rate, the proceeds of same to be spent on the upkeep of the Library in building to be donated. A' site must al,•o be provided without burdening the penny irate. Respectfully yours, James Bertram, secretary." That was less than the coatruittee anticipated, and they decided to make a further representation to Mr. Carnegie. They wrote him pointing out that the corn- mittbe's scheme was based upon the principle of two branoh libraries, that if they proceeded to establish a single building they felt that they must do so without any prospect of being able to afterwards add the second which was contemplated, and that one branch libraary would have to be placed in such a situation and planned on such a scale as would enable it to serve the whole town. They respectfully asked Mr. Carnegie if he could increase his donation. They received the following polite answer, dated August 31st, but unfortunately it I did not go quite so far as they would have liked " Mr. Carnegie does not see his way to add to the amount promised for a branch building and he does not agree with you that the fact of erecting a moderate- sized branch building now should in any way block the erection of another branch building in another district later on." The sum of £3,500 was to be spent on the building, the maintenance to come out of the rates. if the General Purposes Committee were prepared to pro- vide a site then the Library Committee— The MAYOR : You might have approached them and asked them. I should withdraw this and send it to the General Purposes Committee, and let them consider it. Councillor PEMBERTON pointed out that they were only asked to accept the offer provisionally. The MAYOR : I think I. as head of the Corporation, ought to know something about it. If this is passed ' it will not need to go to the committee. Alderman BEN.iErr said that in face of the charge which had been made against the committee that they were keeping matters back, they were only doing their duty in acquainting the Council with the position of affairs. He was laying the facts before them at he earliest possible moment. He had no doubt that one of the first things that would come before the General Purposes Committee of the new Council would be this question. , Councillor PEMBERTON seconded the adoption of the

The MA.YOR said if they accepted the resolution it meant that the committee would inform Mr. Carnegie that they had accepted his offer. The General Purposes Committee, the Mayor and the Town Clerk ought to have been consulted. The matter had not been done in the way it should have been, and it was that sort of thing that led to serious accusations. Councillor BURTON said the matter seemed to have been rushed, and they ought to have a little more time to consider it to justify them in passing the resolution. In answer to Councillor Burton, the TOWN CLERK said they were entitled to raise a lid. rate for Library purposes under a local Act, and a Ad. rate under the Museums and Gymnasium Act. He believed they were raising 1 W. for Library purposes, but out of that they were paying for the Museum. Councillor EVANS said if they passed the resolution it meant Mr. Carnegie's offer was practically accepted and they were putting on a farthing rate. Councillor LORD thought a wrong impression would be conveyed to Mr. Carnegie if the resolution was referred back. They were committed to nothing by accepting the minutes in their present form. They wanted to see what this was going to cost the ratepayers. They might have another lid. rate put upon them. Councillor PEMBERTON said they could not raise another lid rate. The matter was keenly debated in the com- mittee. They had to give Mr. Carnegie some answer to his letter. It was only courteous to do so. They simply wrote saying that they accepted his offer provisionally. The proper reply to have sent to the millionaire was that they had received his letter and that the matter was being discussed by the various committees' and by so doing they would not have been committed to any- thing. Alderman BENNETT said the amendment was due to the Mayor's super-sensitiveness because he was jealous for the authority of the committee. He had imagined an affront where none was intended. The object of the Museum Committee had been to free itself from the charge of undue secretiveness and to take the public into its confidence, and it was an unusual thing to be blamed for a thing which was often said to be wanting in that Council. The Council was committed to nothing. He should have been glad if 11r. Carnegie had made his gift more, but they could not dictate to these gentlemen. They suggested to Mr. Carneigie that he should increase his offer, but he told them that he could not do that. It was a question of taking £3,300 or leaving it. Coun- cillor Wilkinson had allowed his imagination to run riot and when be said the Library might cost another lid, rate it was evident that be did not know what he was talking about. (Laughter.) Those who had abused the Museum Committee should read the annual report of Mr. Madeley, and if they did so they would find that there was no doubt that the institution was serving a useful purpose, and was well and efficiently managed. TOWN COUNCIL.

CURIOUS INCIDENT AT THE CEMETERY.

Charging of School Fees.

The final meeting of the Warrington Town Council for the municipal year was held on Tuesday evening at the Town Hall, when there were present :—The Mayor (Alderman Bolton), Aldermen A. Bennett, John Evans, R. W.Francomb, J. Smethurst, D. Tinnion, Councillors T. H. Ashcroft, J. Ball, T. Barton, W. Benson, T. Burton, T. Chantler, W. Clegg, J. G. Craik, E. Cunnington, J. Dickenson, D. S. Duncan, James Evans, L. Ellison, Dr. Joseph, J. Lord, J. Pemberton, J. Sankey, J. Skellancl, J. F. Smith, F. Stott. G. T. Wilding, J. Wilkinson, and W.J. Wilson, J.L. Whittle (town clerk), A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (bbrougb treasurer), T. Longdin (borough surveyor), J. Dees (water engineer), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), F. G. Ruddock (borough analyst).

SCHOOL FEES AT THE " BEAMONT " SCHOOL. Councillor BALI. moved as an amendment to the minutes of the Education Committee Vaal the resolu- tion of the Education Committee relating to the charg- ing of school fees at" Beamont " school be not con- firmed. It seemed to him that the imposition of school fees in a rate-provided school was altogether outside the intention of the Act. The cost of education should be borne by the rates. He had made inquiries in several places, and it seemed to him to be against the spirit of educational progress to charge school fees in rate-provided schools. (Hear, hoar.) Councillor STOTT seconded, and said he quite agreed with Councillor Ball that the schools onght to be entirely free. The cost at least of elementary education ought to be borbe by the whole community. He did not think that the Council ought to allow the Education Committee to ask the sanction of the Local Government Board to give them permission to charge fees in the " Beamont " school. They ought to take the first step at that juncture to free the whole of the schools. A number of the schools in the Borough were already free. Alderman BENNETT said he was in entire sympathy with the underlyinr, sentiment of the mover and seconder of the amendment, but he was unable to accept their practical conclusions. He thought there was a great deal to be said for making the whole of the schools free, and he should find it difficult to oppose a resolution of that kind if it was a well considered scheme. it was, however, a mistake to go into the matter piece-meal. The proper way to go into the ques- tion was to consider the whole as a part of a well- considered policy. (Hear, hear.) The INlAvoil rtged the discussion out of order, as this was not a question of expending money, but of collect- ing it.

BRICKLAYERS' WAGES.

Councillor STOTT raised the question of bricklayers' wages at the same school. It was twelve months since a protest was made about the conditions which obtained in regard to the building of the school. The Maron : The resolution says that the Com- mittee are satisfied. Councillor STOTT : But lan not on the Committee. Councillor PENIBERTON said the Council had power to settle the matter. Councillor STOTT said that what he claimed was that any labourer who was supplying a bricklayer with material was a bricklayer's labourer and ought to be paid Gd. per hour. (Hear, hear). Councillor BA.LL seconded. Alderman BENNE 17 contended that the Masters' Federation ought certainly to know something about the matter. He had no personal objection to the matter being referred back and would withdraw it.

WHO TOLD? NO INFORMATION TO BE OBTAINED.

The Council was then called upon to consider the re- port of the special committee appointed "to investigate report which appeared in the ‘Varrington Guardian ' of the ]sth September, 1906. relating to the acticn pending between the Corporation and Colonel Edelsten." The report, which we published last week stated that the committee had failed to discover who supplied the information.

Its adoption was moved by Councillor WILKINSON and seconded by Councillor BARTON. Alderman TINNION said the matter was too important to pass without some explanation from the chairman of the committee. There was evidently still the accusation against some official or member of the Council, and yet they were attempting to dispose of this matter in a manner altogether different from any other on which reports had to be made. He moved that the report be 'not confirmed until the Council had had a copy of the evidence taken by the Inveatigation Committtee, or a fuller explanation. Councillor PENIIBERToN thought that was unnecessary. The committee +rad given an enormous amount of time to the work. Alderman Tinnion had an opportunity of being on the committee, but he refused. He was pre- pared to give £lO to the infirmary If Alderman Titinion could get to know who gave the information. Councillor WILKINSON, chairman of the Investigation Committee, agreed that the information given in their report was meagre, but it was impossible for them to make a better one. After examining every official and every member of the officials staff who might have had an opportunity of divulging the information, they, as a last resort, went to the "Guardian " 0 lice, and were assured that it was against principle to divulge the sonrces of their information. The manager led them to believe that it was not an official.

The report was approved. STREET DIPROVENIENT.

ACQUIRING PROPERTY IN RYLANDS STREET. At a meeting of the Street Improvement and. Light- ing Committee the Town Clerk reported that he had served notice to treat, upon the executors of the late Thomas Hewitt for the purchase of premises in and near Rylands-street. It was resolved that the land left after widening the new street to 20 feet be fenced off. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee it was resolved that in the event of the executors of allowing immediate possess- em interest after the si Thomason,CorporationtheHew i Hewitt, de c easwe 1, l p a y rate of 5 per cent per annum upon the amount of the purchase money until the time that such purchase money be agreed upon or ascertained by arbitration and that the common seal be affixed to such agreement. In tF e alternative the Town Clerk be instructed to take the necessary Steps under the Land Clauses Acts for the obtaining of possession and that the common seal be affixed to the necessary bond. Councillor PENIBERTON daring a discussion on the minutes, said he noticed that plans for additions to pre- mises in Sankey-street for Mr.T.Birtles were recommend- ed for approval. They had found it necessaryto have a 20-feet street behind property in Bridge-street, and he could not understand why they did not have an agree- ment with Mr. Birtles for at least a ten feet passage behind his property. The minutes were. approved. In moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Committee, Ald- erman BMETT referred to the state of the tramway track in the town. He understood that the Electricity and Tramways Committee were having fresh contractors for the work. STRANGE INCIDENTS AT TEIE CEMETERY.

A—t'a meeting of the Cemetery Committee the Town Clerk reported that the Registrar had reported to him a mistake made by two gravediggers whereby the bodies of two children were interred in the wrong graves, and that he had applied to the Home Secretary for an order to re-inter in the right graves. In accordance with instructions received at the last meeting, the Town Clerk submitted the evidence collected by him as to the burial of a pauper whose body had been found in a stable at Hollins Green, from which it appeared that the main cause of complaint, viz. : that there was no intention of holding a burial service was not sustained by the complainant. The Committee, however, were of opinion that the undertaker had not acted properly in placing the body in the mortuary without informing the Registrar thereof. The undertaker was called in, and after explaining his action, was regnested not to again adopt the course he had taken in this instance. It was resolved that the Board of Guardians be informed that this Committee will not in future accept for burial the bodies of paupers dying is districts out- side the Borough: and that the Town Clerk approach the authorities at Winwiek Asylum with a view to ascer- taining whether they intend to make provision for a burial ground there. Alderman EV ASS, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that from some unaccountable cause the bodies of two children were taken to the wrong graves. This occurred on the Friday and :qr. Young was not made aware of the mistake until the following Thursday when the parent of one of the children informed him. Immediately application was made to the Home Office and with the authorties' consent, the bodies were taken up and re-interred in the presence of relatives and friends. With regard to the Hollins Green affair, the body was found on the Sunday and was brought to the Warrington Cemetery on the following Wednesday at eight o'clock in the evening. Owing to a roller having been broken the gat eis could not he shut, and the under- taker who was the contractor to the Warrington Union brought the body there and without telling anyone took it into the Cemetery. The mortuary door was broken open and the body was left in the building. The officials heard nothing of the matter until a man went to the mortuary and found the corpse there. The undertaker turned up on Thursday and said the body was in such a dreadful state that he would not take any responsibility. He refused to let his men do anything but carry the corpse to the grave. On Friday the body was placed in a grave and a relative, on finding this out, took it for granted that there was to be no religious ceremony. Asa matter of fact, the service did take place. The com- mittee complained of the action of the undertaker in placing jibe body in the mortuary without giving notice to anyone and he was plainly told that such thing must not occur again. With regard to the,' burial of bodies from Winwick Asylum, when the authorities first applied to the Committee for,

MEM

permission to bury their dead in the Cemetery it was agreed that they should do so on payment of I double fees. From March 31st, 1903, to March 190 G, there had been no fewer than 513 interments from the Asylum, the average being 117 per annum and 13.25 per month. The Committee felt the responsibility was too great for them to undertake to carry on the agreement any further. The Cemetery would be filled long before they anticipated, and in the near future they would be having to apply for an extension of the burial ground. Councillor STOTT said that with regard to the Efollins Green case, the relatives were told the funeral would take place at three o'clock on Friday afternoon. They went to the Cemetery at 2.15 and found, to their amaze- ment, that the coffin was in the grave, and the under- taker was about to go away. They naturally formed tile impression that because the man was a pauper there was to be no service. He (Councillor Stott) was satisfied that there was no such intention, and he would like to bear testimony to the excellent way in which the Assistant Town Clerk had investigated the case. The minutes were approved. A PARK-KEEPER'S SICK PAY. " PHNALISED FOR THRIFT."

It having been reported to the Town Hall, Parks and Batbs Committee that James Bramhall, park-keeper at Victoria Park, was ill and in receipt of club pay, it was resolved that he be allowed Gs. per week for one month. At a later meeting it was reported that Bramhall had died. This committee also resolved at the request of the Street Improvement Committee that trees be planted in VVinwick-street and Church-street. Councillor STorr said it seemed to him that the man Bramhall had been penalized because he ha l been thrifty and belonged to friendly societies. The MAYOR said the Committee were helping the friendly societies by seeing that they were not put on by men receiving big wages and drawing their sick pay longer than they should do. Councillor STOTT said that if an official was ill, the Council would not ask if he was in a friendly society but would give him his full salary. The NIAYOR said the Corporation were not supposed to give any sick pay to workmen. It was an act of kindness on the Committee's part to help the man when he was ill. •

•The niinutes were approved. MORE ABOUT THE "WATER LILY."

The Water Committee resolved that the tender of Messrs. Death and Ellwood, of Leicester, for the repair accordinz, to specification of the Beam Engine " Water Lily " at Winwick Pumping Station at .£6BO Lis. be accepted. The Committee discussed the proposal to clean out the Appleton Reservoir and after fully considering the matter it was decided not to pro- ceed at present with the work. Alderman FRANCOMB moved the adoption of the mi nutes.

Alderman EVANS said he would like to call attention to the fact that the cost of fuel for pumping at Winwick was 53. 9d. per million gallons whereas at Delph- lane it was only 103. He thought those figures spoke for themselves as to the policy of repairing the engine. Counciilor WILDING said the real question was as to the life of the respective engines. The " Water Lily " would last much longer than the engine at Dolph-lane. The MA.YOR. : It will Cost twice as much before it ass done. . .

Councillor STOTT thought that in view of the figures submitted by Alderman Evans the Couucil would have been better advised if they had adhered to their original decision to compound the engine. The saving in the price of fuel would eventually have psid for the cost of the work. _ .

It was stated that a Warrington firm tendered for the work but at h higher figure. Alderman BENN mrr said he would like to call the attention of those who were averse to municipal trading to the fact that during the past year the water works made a gross profit of over £16,000. That allowed for all the expendi- ture on the extension scheme to date and showed a return of 4 per cent, on the capital outlay. It was interesting to know, in face of thra allegations that were flying over the town, that the liabilities on the undertaking amounted to £400,000 while the assets totalled £450,000. Councillor PEMBERTON pointed out that the engine at Delph-lane cost the Corporation £12,000 and there was no possible comparison between that engine and the old Water Lily." He failed to see that the figures were a fair test, for if they were it would be a question of whether the Corporation should not scrap "all the engines at Winwick. After further discussion the minutes were approved. THE TRAM TRACK.

At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee, a letter was read from the Stockton Heath Parish Council asking the Council to support them in their complaint lodged with the 11Ianchester Ship Canal ' Company against the unnecessary delay in the working ' of the '1 wenty steps Bridge. As the committee had received several complaints it was decided to write to the Ship Canal Company on the matter.—Tenders for the paving of the tramway track were opened, initialled and referred to the Chairman, Deputy-Chairman and Engineer. Alderman SMETHURST moved the confirmation of the minutes, and said the Committee thought they would save a great deal of money by placing the tram track in a satisfactory condition. Councillor WILKE...No:4 ssconded.

Alderman TININION said in some places the tram track was highly dangerous. Councillor STOTT moved the recommendation be referred back as he did not think the committee should have obtained tenders without the consent of the Council. The Tramways Committee and Paving Com- mittee ought to be compelled to agree on matters of this kind.

Councillor LORD was of opinion that the Paving Committee should take the matter entirely out of the hands of the Tramways Committee. In the course of a low, discussion Alderman SMETHURST said the Paving Committee's tenders would be considered if they competed in the ordinary way. He maintained that the tramway track was as good as any in Lancashire. The amendment was carried by a large majority.

THE EDELSTEN ACTION.

A meeting of the General Purposes Committee was convened for the purpose of considering the defence and counterclaim of the Corporation in the action of Edelsten v. the Corporation and Bolton. The Deputy Town Clerk explained the position of the Corporation, and after discussion it was resolved that the defence and counterclaim now submitted be approved. At this stage the Mayor left the chair snd Mr. Coun- cillor Evans was appointed to preside. It having been explained that a formal resolution authorising the defence of the action was necessary, and that certain proceedings had of necessity been taken, without such rnolution it was resolved, that the action of the Town Clerk in defending the action be approved, and that he take all the necestary steps for the defence of the Cor- poration. At another meeting the question of an application as to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £34,059 us. 6d., the purchase money of properties bought for street improvements was again considered and deferred.

The -minutes were approved. THANKS TO THE MAYOR.

Alderman FRANCOMR moved :—" That the best thanks of this Council he presented to William Bolton, Esq., J.P., for the satisfactory manner in which he has dis- charged the responsible duties devolving upon him as Mayor and Chief Magistrate of the Borough during this his third year of office, and for the unvarying courtesy and impartiality displayed by him when presiding over the deliberations of the Council, and in his intercourse with its members." The Alderman said the Mayor had devoted an enormous amount of time to the duties of his office. He had spent more time on the various com- mittees than any Mayor who had preceded him. Ho had had a lot of difficulties to contend with, but, on the whole, he had treated the members of the Council with a good deal of kindness, and kept histemper wonder- filly well. (Laughter.) They ought not to take notice of any little failings they might have imagined. He had certainly done his duty. (Hear, hear.) The Alder- man paid a high compliment to the Mayoress, and said Alderman Bolton would not have been such a good Mayor but for his wife. (Applause.) Alderman TINNION seconded, and said some of the members no doubt thought they had been somewhat harshly treated by the Mayor, but they never thought how harshly they had treated the chair. (Hear, hear, and laughter). He considered the Mayor had discharged Ihis duties with very great satisfaction, considering the difficult team he had to drive and manage—(laughter)— and he had done it better than almost any other man would have done. He heartily endorsed all that had been said about the Mayoress. She was a charming I lady, and of a sweet disposition. (Applause). The motion was heartily carried. The MAYOR in reply, said he had tried to do his best. !He had been blamed for givitig latitude, and also for not giving it—(laughter)—but be had done what he considered to he the best in the interest of the rate- payers and of the town generally. If it had not been for the Mayoress he would not have been the Mayor. He could net have had a better helpmeet, and he did not think there was a better Mayoress anywhere, (Applause).

Taxation of Land Values. ! At a meeting of the Warrington members of the English League for the Taxation of Land Values on Friday last week it was unanimously resolved to form a Warrington Branch of the League with Mr. W. H. Sherhurn as president, Mr. Lewsey, hon. treasurer,and Mr. R. Ockel, hon. secretary. Ladies are eligible for membership, and it was further resolved to hold on alternate Fridays meetings to which the general public will be invited.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."] There has been nothing half-hearted about Warring- ton's progress in the Lancashire Cup Competition and on Saturday they qualified for the semi-final by trouncing Barrow to the extent of 47 points to 14. The visitors made a good fight for two-thirds of the game but collapsed completely later, and Warrington quickly doubled their score, at one period of the game 20 points being put on in 8 minutes. The brilliance which has characterised Warriftgton's play in all home matches was again apparent, and hopes are enter - tamed that the Cup will find a home in Warrington before the year is out.

Barrow failed to maintain the reputation they have ea vned in League matches. They showed pace and smartness at times,hut were no match for the Warring- ton men. For one thing,they were too light physically and the powerful home forwards at times simply scattered the opposition. A great misfortune befel the visitors early on in losing Gifford, their clever full back, and this had doubtless much to do with the score reaching such mammoth proportions. Most of the Warrington men had a hand in the scoring, and it is going to be a close thing by the end of the season between the for- wards and backs for the top score.

The spectators must admit that they got full value for their money on Saturday, for the "A" team wiped the floor with Salford " A " prior to the cup-tie. In the two matches over 100 points were scored, the Warrington men aggregating 82 'points against the opposition's 20 points. Not a bad afternoon's work !

On the Barrow side, Bowker and Fox were best on the attack, but the latter was woefully weak in defence. In the second half he gave three tries away through failing eta critical moment. This' is not twit° the class form which we were led to expect from Fox.

! On Wednesday, Warrington were again engaged this time at Salford in a League match. Sad to relate they did not rise to the occasion, and sustained their second defeat of the season. The ball was very wet and heavy, and this handicapped all efforts at com- bination, which wee distinctly poor on both sides. Somehow Warrington could not adapt themselves to the changed conditions, and nobody would have sus- pected that they were the same team which has per- formed so brilliantly of late. Salford played with rare dash and made up for lack of polish by energy and determination. They had quite as much of the game as their opponents, but they did not deserve such a lucky win as fell to their lot.

Warrington were really beaten by the Referee, and a lot of dissatisfaction was felt at the close. Half way through the second part of the game the teams were level with the score five points each. Warrington had a good chance of winning and were showing better form than before, when a sensational, incident occurred. Lomas got away at centre with the ball at his toes and kicked over the Warrington line. In the race for the ball Taylor just beat the Salford man and made a very fine save. Then the referee came up and to Warrington's consternation ruled that Taylor had impeded Lomas and awarded a try to Salford. What Taylor.could have done otherwise is a mystery. He had to save his side, and could not be expected to stand still while Lomas made the winning point. Surely a defending player is allowed to shoulder another man off the ball in Rugby football? This is the most that Taylor did,and not only so he fairly won the race for possession. For mane, I think Referee VCatcheon was wrong. He was half the length of the field away at the time, and should have thought twice before giving such a momentous decision. Of course, it won Salford the match. Warrington were further unlucky in having an apparently fair try denied to Thomas i when he took Cartwright's pass and ran n under the posts in the last five minutes of the game.

It is rather curious that in the only two matches Warrington have lost this season Isherwood has been absent. There is no doubt that-the presence of the popular centre has a big effect on the team. For ono thing Fish does not get the work he should have when Isherwood is missing, and this was noticeable at SalforA. Cartwright did not play badly hut he has not yet got the knack that" Danny " has of seizing an opportunity and setting Fish in motion for the line. More than one of the Warrington men was decidedly below form on Wednesday. Taylor was most erratic and could scarcely hold the ball either from ft pass or a punt. Consequently Brooks was more idle than Fish and never got a chance. Berry played anything but a good game, and was more than once out of place when the ball dropped. In fact Salford had much the best of the kicking, and were also superior forward. The Warrington six showed a slackness in the first half quite unusual for them. Even in the loose Sal- ford were snlarter and their following up was one of the features of the game. D. Preston played a grand game at half back, and was well backed up by Lomas at centre and Mac Whirter at full back. As was the case last season Lomas got both the Salford tries against Warrington, though one was a gift on Wed- desday. Hockenhall tried hard to put a better com- plexion on the gam?, but Lees was limping badly and could give -little sttport.

The draw for the semi-finals for the Lancashire Cup has again been favourable to Warrington who will be at home to Oldham and should make no mis- take about recording a victory. Runcorn have to go to Broughton, where they will probably make a good fight, but the likelihood is that the finvl will rest with Warrington and the Rangers. The semi-finals are to be played on Saturday, November 17th.

There should be one of the tit-bits of the season on the Warrington ground this afternoon when Wigan are due as visitors. Unfortunately the fixture clashes with the County match at Weaste and Wigan will be without Leytham and Jenkins at threequatter, while Warrington are deprived of Naylor's services. The Wigan Club is strong in reserves, however, and War- rington will have to show vastly better form than on Wednesday to record a substantial victory. The selected Warrinaton team shows one or two changes, and is as follows; Berry, full back ; cish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Brooks, threequarters ; Hocken- hall and Jordan, half backs ; Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Harmer and Shugars, forwards.

The "A " team play their combination fixture at Wigan, and should possess a fair chance of winning, with the following teAna :--Knight, full back ; Lambert, Jones, Cartwright, and Landor, three- quarters ; Lloyd and Kay, half backs ; Boardman, Jolley, Swift, Belton, Forster and Bradshaw, forwards.

-- " Nnnquam " writes me a line of complaint about the work of the Warrington foEwards in the scrum- mages, and asserts that opposing teams—with the exception of Huddersfield—get possession CO per cent more than the Warrington men. There is certainly ground for the comulaint, and there are likely to be a few changes in the front rank unless the work in the pack comes up to the level of that done in the loose.

LOCAL CHURCHES.

Services for Sunday. Bold-street Wesleyan Church : Preacher, 10,30 and 6.30, Rev. T. Galland Hartley,Chairman of the District. Special music will be given by the choir. Baptist Church, Latchford : Annual Missionary Services : preachers, 10.30, Mr. Stephen Ackerley ; 6.0, Rev. E. C. Smyth (China). Baptist Church, Golborne-street : Missionary Anni- versary ; preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Mr. J. Harrison, Liverpool. In the afternoon the annual missionary meeting will be held. Mr. B. Few will preside and an address will he given by the Rev. C. E. Smyth. Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church : Preachers, 10.35 Rev. John Gunnell ; 6.30, Rev. E. J. G.iwne. Wycliffe Congregational Church: The Rev. John Yonge, who celebrates the thirtieth anniversary of his ministry, will preach both morning and evening. Golborne-street Baptist Church. The Bible Class anniversary in connection with the above church was held on Sunday, the preacher being the Rev. G. J. Cliff, evangelist, of Loughborough. In the morning the rev, gentleman took for his text, " The Book of Books "and in the evening " A Fatal Flaw." At the evening service a quartette was rendered, and solos were given in an admirable manner by Miss E. Barnes and Mr. G. Gough. In the afternoon, Alderman , Tinnion presided over a musical service. Selections were rendered in a pleasing style by Miss L. Hewitt (soprano), Miss L. Fairhurst (contralto), Mr. J. Corrin (tenor), and Mr. E. Walker (baritone). Mr. A. Trow accompanied and Mr. S. Rickers presided at the organ. Large congregations assembied at each service. The collections were in aid of the trust funds. On Thursday evening an ivy social arranged by the young ladies was held, when about 150 spent a very enjoyable evening. Solos were given by Miss L. Hewitt and Miss K. Hamer. Tho schoolroom was very taste- fully decorated with ivy. The young ladies who had the arrangements in hand had also prettily adorned their dresses with ivy and chrysanthemums. The pro- ceeds were in aid of the Church Funds. Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

ISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY. The foreign missionary anniversary services in con- nection with the above church were held on Sunday and were well attended. The pulpit was occupied in the morning by the Rev. E. J. Gawne, who took for his subject, "Ye are my witnesses." In the afternoon children's service was held, when the Bev. E. J awn ano again wasaindealt the Rethaly.isll The preacher in the e Annual Meeting. The annual missionary meeting which was well attended was held in the church on Wednesday evening, Mr; J. G. Withinshaw, senior circuit steward, presided and at the outset promised to give £1 for every £?, realized by the collection. He urged a greater gener- osity in the matter of subscribing to the funds of the foreign n119310133.—The Rev. C. H.Hocken,who formerly was a minister of the \Warrington Circuit, and who had a very hearty reception, pleaded for additional help to forward the work in India. Before the days of Columbus nations had struggled with each other for the possession of India. Did they think there waS no finger of Providence in the fact that the struggle had resulted in the possession of India lsy the foremost Protestant country in the world and not by any Papal power? We had given India our glorious literature, and science but we must give also the knowledge of Jesus Christ. The gospel was spreading in India and there was one thin," which must cause the church much thought, and that was that the church which was doing the most of India was the Nonconformist Church of America. The Rev.E.Dewstoe who has recently returned from China, gave an inter- esting address upon his work. China was waking up in a most amazing fashion. The whole country was full of signs and omens which they could not understand. In all matters except one there was an impassable gulf between the E tst and West. The exception was the Gospel of JeStl9 Christ and for the futute peace of the world it behoved then to spread the Gospel. (Hear, hear). A collection was taken on behalf of foreign missions.

-But—on, neaven !—but 1 oh not love you." "Pooh! Don't repeat that nonsense so often. i merely ask for your hanid in marriage, and trust. to making you love me in time. give me your answer." There was an interval of tense silence. And then a sob came to Ashleigh's ears, and Miss Gresham broke out 'wildly: "Leave me—leave me! Do not be such a coward and brute as to force an answer from me now. Give me more time—you must give me snore—and you know that I love my mother so dearly as to save her if possible. 'Leave me 1201 T —at once." "Very well, very well," growled her com- panion after a long pause. "But." he added. menacingly, I snail come again on this day week at twelve o'clock, and then, if you don't give me the answer I expect-- Well, good-night now, Eva. Go indoors at once, and take a sensible view of things. Good-night." With that he strode away, and immediate)/ afterwards his trap went down the drive at a rapid pace. But before the sound of the grinding wheels and clattering hoofs had died away into silence, Ashleigh had risen and hobbled towards Miss Gresham. She did not seem startled at seeing him. She looked at him for a moment with dumb agony in her eyes and then turned away from him, and, lean- ing more heavily on the sun-dial, sighed deeply. I "Miss Gresham, I know lour trouble. Have no fear, for I may shield you from it." She turned to him, instantly reviving from her semMaint, and eagerly caught him by the arm. "Oh, Mr. Herbert—Mr. Herbert! What do you say? But, ah! you cannot save me." "I can," he answered in a firm undertone. And then he put his strong arm round her pro- teetingly, as though she were a child. "Eva, I love you, and I shall save you, whether you will have me or not. Come in now, my dear; the night is chilly." And then the lame man led her towards the house. But they had not yet got to the window when, I with a sudden movement, she slid her arm round I his neck, and in the moonlight raised her tear- stained face to his. With tender reverence he kissed her then. On the following Sunday Mr. Cooper-Ward arrived before noon at Peacock Farm. His manner was th,at of a man who would stand no nonsense, and it did not belie his intentions. But when he asked for an interview alone with Miss Gresham, that young lady displayed a spirit which took him back, and insisted on her mother ica-saing a. thiuJ

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING. Warrington A.C. and Sutton Harriers met on Satur- day' at Warrington. The National Champions were weakened by the absence of Whittle, Wilding and Wilcox. Booth and Rimmer (Warrington) laid a good trail over seven miles of heavy country and a race through was agreed upon. Th.l pack, consisting of 22 Warrington and 17 Sutton, were despatched to a good start and the home team were soon well to the front. Jas. Parsonage (Warrington), ran splendidly, and was first home, followed by J. Morris (Sutton), and then followed seven more of the home team, the race resulting in an easy win for Warrington by 44 points. Placings : WARRINGTON. SUTTON. J. Parsonage S. Parsonage H. K. Jepson H. DRESS—FASHION—HOMe. THE GIPSY'S PROPHECY. encounter between the Queen of Spain, when she was a young girl, and a fortune-teller is related in 31. _I. P. The Princess was walk- ing near Osborne with her brother when they met an old gipsy woman, who, unaware of the identity of the children, asked for alms. The Princess fumbled in her pocket and extracted her only coin—a shilling—and handed it to the woman, who asked in gratitude: "Would the young lady like her fortune told?" The Prin- cess held out her hand. "The young lady has great good fortune in store," said the gipsy, examining it. "She will turn from Cross towards Cross; she will make a journey by sea to marry a dark man-k-the man of her heart; she will 'rule amongst rulers; she will see death, but shall live unscathed; and she will bear a eon, and all before a score of years have passed over her head." FILETS DE SAIIMON A LA REINE:--Make a mar- inade of the juice of one lemon, one small onion finely chopped, one teaspoonful of anchovy sauce, one blade of mace—pounded— and one gill of white wine. Lay the fillets of salmon in the marinade, and leave to soak for two hours. Then drain, dip in flour, brush over with a beaten egg, sift lightly with flour, and fry in boiling fat a good colour. When cola strain. Take a tablesrtoonful of finely-minced parsley, and mix with •the yolks of three hard- boiled eggs rubbed to a paste with a dessert- spoonful of salad oil, a dessertspoonful of the marinade liquor, and a tablespoonful of chopped caners. Stir in half a nint of stiffly-beaten cream, stand on ice, and when firm cut into neat blocks with a sharp knife. Place one in the centre of each fillet, and garnish with rings bf hard-boiled egg and four prawns to each sal. mom fillet. WASTING OF THE NERYES. Invalid Nearly Flve Years. TWO SPECIALISTS FAILED. " Yes ; but she has sokne vrry eccen- tric ideas." " Indeed?" " Yes. She enters our messenger-boy's wages as 'running ex- penses.'" "John, have you got everything?" tenderly inquired the billionaire's wife, as he started off on a journey. The billionaire burst into tears. "There yougo I" he exclaimed; "always saying things to give me pain. You know very well that, in spite of all my efforts, I haven't yet suc- ceeded in getting everything." It appears that there is a rule in the Civil Ervice restricting the amount of leave which is to be given to each officer. A prominent official asked for leave of absence for one day to enable hini to be present at the funeral of his wife's mother. The application was passed in the ordinary course to his superior officer, who submitted it to the chief of the department, with the following ambiguous comment: " Sub- mitted and recommended. Mr. -- has not had a similar privilege in the past twelve months." Uncle: Is your father still quarrelling with the neighbour because his cat dined on your canary?" Nephew: "Oh, no; that is all over long ago." Uncle: "Wells I'm glad to hear your father finally buried the hatchet." Nephew: But he didn't bury the hatchet, uncle; he buried the cat." " I tell you," said the young sub-editor of a local paper, that the editor isn't in, and rm not going to tell you again. If you have any- thing for him you can leave it with me." "Very well," said the caller, taking off his coat. " I came in to give him a good, souriff thrashing, but I'll give it to you instead." Old Gentleman: "Don't you find this work rather monotonous?" Lift Boy: " No, indeed, sir; it's very exciting. Only yesterday a man started to get out too soon and got his head cracked; and last week the machine broke and crippled everybody except me. That there rope looks sort o' weak. Hope she'll last till were up. The engineer is ill to-day. too, and that makes things more interesting still." " But, John, I'm afraid it won't go with the tradesmen." At an election meeting recently the candidate was somewhat troubled by a man in the audi- ence, who at length became offensively personal. " Is it true that your mother washes—" he began; but before he could add the word "clothes " the witty candidate called out smartly: " Of course she does; why don't you?" This raised a loud laugh at the disturber's ex- pense; but-still undaunted, he returned to the attack. " You can't deny,"he said. " that your father was a rag-and-bone man. I bought some old clothes of him thirty years ago." And I see you're still wearing them !" was the candi- date's lightning retort. Stern Parent: ".From my observation of him lad night, I came to the conclusion that that young man of yours was rather wild." Daugh- ter : 'Of course. It was your constant obser- vation that made him wild. He wanted you to go upstairs, and leave us alone." "By the way, Bishop, why is it that you always address your congregation as 'Brethren' and never mention the women in your ser- mons?" "But my dear madam, the Cite em- braces the other.' "Oh, but, Bishop, not in church !" The A— Volunteers were in camp, and one of their officers had come suddenly upon a private wending his way towards a tent with a fine rooster in his arme. The officer halted him to know if he had been stealing. "No, sir." was the reply. "I just saw the old fellow sitting on a wall. and I ordered him to crow for old England, and as he wouldn't I confiscated him for a rebel." -- First Man: "Do you know what Burrows is doing just now?" Second Man: "Oh, he's travelling with a circus." First Man "Ah ! That will be hard work, I should think." Second Man: "No; all he's got to do is to stick his head in the lion' 13 mouth twice a day." The "voices" at an election meeting are often most disconcerting. "Man," said he, "I dinna ken; I'm following the flittin'." He wan cleaning his skates and whistling merrily when his mother remarked :—"The paper this morning has an account of a little boy who was drowned while skating on thin ice." The boy ceased whistling, but kept on rubbing one of the skates. "Too had !" said he. "I wonder if he was any relation to the boy who was killed the day before yesterday by a tram- car while on his way to school?" Something glittered in the mud. It was a Pin. "See a pin, and pick it up. and all day long you'll hare good luck," muttered young Jephson, as he started out to get, the pin. He had learned that old saying when quite a child. As he bent down to reach the pin, hisPhat fell off, and rolled puder a cab-wheel. His spec- fades aLio fell, and broke on the hard road. A 'bus almost ran over him on one side, and baker's van on the other. In making a dash for the pavement, he upset a cyclist, who at that moment was wheeling round the far side' of a tram. Altogether it was a great adventure— but he had got the pin. The couple who got into the tramcar were obviously man and wife, and just as obviously in the midst of a domestic dispute. As the lady settled' herself in her seat, she exclaimed bit- terly: "There is no calamity that can befall a woman that I have not suffered." "There you are wrong, Jane," the husband replied. "You have never been a widow." Looking at him with a frow, she retorted: "I said 'calamity,' sir." -- Home Seeker : "What miserably imperfect glass you have in these windows ! Dealer: "Antique, madam? There's no doubt about that. Why, it was so worm-eaten when I bought it, that I have had to have a new back, a new seat, and three new legs made for it!"

Cake—and why not? You can bake it yourself if you buy We are informed that a specially convened meeting of the National Council of the Evangelical Free IP 1 9 Churches will be held to consider the Education Question, on Monday next, November sth, in London. Seen by a representative of our paper, the Rev. Thomas Law explained why it had been thought necessary to take such a serious step. "It was anticipated, at our regular meeting at Birmingham in March, that circumstances might arise in connection with the Education Question to render Cake Flour \ necessary the meeting together again of the whole assembly before the regular meetings, which will bo 31d. a packet of all held in March next at Leeds, and a resolution wa k bakers and grocers. passed giving the Executive power to call such a Illustrated cookery book free gathering. Of course only a case of most vital neces- \ \ on application to Dept. sity will allow the Executive to act upon this resolu- tion, but the officers consider the situation at the Paul Bros., Millers, rg, Bruns- present time so grave as to render this imperative. wick Street, Liverpool. 42 You see, the Covernment have gone as far as they could go in tbe way of concessions , in the House of Commons, farther than many Free. Churchmen think justifiable. Now there is serious danger that the House of Lords will insert into the measure alterations that will make it quite impossible for us to accept. The amendments already tabled by Use Archbishop of Canterbury would sweep away the whole foundation of the Government rneasure,and make the Bill in our eyes even worse than the Act of 1902. This will, of course, be the first time in the history of the movement that the National Council have been specially summoned/ and only the greatest possible urgency would impel us to take such a course." I supose the meeting will be really representative of the whole country? "Most decidealy,' Mr. Law replied. "We only sent out the communication convening the Council on Friday last, and by Monday over 700 had reported their intention of being present, and further batches of acceptances are coming in every day. These representa- tives are coming flora places as remote as Nortbumber- landand Penzance. The meeting will be fully repre- sentative of the whole of England and Wales." "As to details, luncheon, provided by the Chairman of our Finance Committee' Mr. J. Compton Rickett M.P., will be served at the Holborn Restaurant to all the members, who will afterwards adjourn to the King's Hall in the same building for the session." "Who will be the speakers, Mr. Law ?" "All the prominent leaders of the various Free Church denominations will be present and take part. The Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., our President, will be in the chair, and whilst I cannot give the speakers, Dr. Clifford, of course, will have a prominent place in pre- senting the resolutions and such important lenders from the provinces as th'e Rev. J.H.Jowett M.A„will be coming up specially to assist."

Show of Racing Pigeons. The first annual show of racing pigeons in connec- tion with the West Lancashire Saturday Federation was held in the Winwick-street Co-operative Hall on Saturday. A large number of entries were received, and some five huudred birds were shown by the principal breeders in the North. Northern Baptist Union. Rev. H. Dunnico on Church and Damocracy The autumnal meetings of the Northern Baptist Union commenced on Monday evening, with a public meeting in Fishergate Baptist Church, Preston. The principal speaker was the Rev. Herbert Dannico, of Kensington, Liverpool. In the course of an address on the signs of the times, the speaker said that we were living in a traditional age. The last fifty years had witnessed a revolution greater than had ever taken place before. History, astronomy, geology, biology, had beeome new. They had a new universe, new men, and also the new woman. The old notions bad been com- pletely upset. It was not merely that they were going. They had gone, and it was impossible to think of the universe and all it contained as men did before the days of Huxley, Spencer, Darwin, Haeckel, and a host of others. Theology was subject to the same laws as other sciences, and they as ministers and laymen must hold themselves in readiness to recon- sider and readjust their methods of thought and work. One of the most conspicuous features of this transitional period was the apparent failure of the Church to interest the democracy. This failure was not one they could complacently ignore. He was no pessimist, but he seriously and solemly appealed to his co-workers to try and remove that reproach from the Church by taking tt more personal interest in the daily life of the people. Let them preach the Gospel of Jesus Christ with all the power and the passion they possessed, but they must remember that religion was for the body as well as the soul, for the nation as well as the individual, for this world as well as the next. One of their leading ministers had said that a democracy that could not work out its own salvation was not worthy of the name, hut such a settlement was com- pletely out of harmony with the words of the Master himself, who came to seek and to save the lost. , 4110.
